Which Airlines Are Dry (No Alcohol)? Can You Bring Your Own Alcohol?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

For many people, alcohol is an aspect of traveling by air that they enjoy. I’m not just talking about first or business class travel, where a glass of champagne might be part of the fun. Airports are also one of the few places where you can have a drink at 5AM and not be judged (too hard).

Admittedly customs differ around the globe, based on culture, religion, etc. In this post, I thought it would be interesting to talk a bit about dry airlines, as there’s a bit of nuance to this.

Which airlines don’t serve alcohol onboard? Which airlines don’t serve alcohol but let you bring your own alcohol? And does all of this even matter?

Let’s start by taking a look at the airlines that are dry, meaning that they don’t serve you alcohol. Here are the airlines that come to mind:

Admittedly that list isn’t totally exhaustive, since there are a countless number of small airlines out there. However, I think this covers the biggest and most interesting airlines that don’t serve alcohol (though if I missed any major ones, please let me know).

There’s an interesting twist to this. While some airlines are dry, they’ll let you bring your own alcohol onboard, and then you can consume it (within reason). The following three international carriers come to mind:

Now, it’s important to note that this isn’t without strings attached:

When I flew Kuwait Airways several years back, I wrote about how we brought a bottle of wine onboard, and they gladly let us consume it. It’s not that I couldn’t be without alcohol, but I was curious to experience a BYOB concept on a plane, since that was novel for me.

There’s an added layer of complexity to all of this, as there are some airlines that aren’t generally dry, but are dry on certain routes. Several situations come to mind (let me know if I’m missing any):

As you can tell, in some of the above situations, the lack of alcohol presumably has little to do with religion or concerns over behavior, and more to do with controlling costs.

People have different takes as to how “bad” it is to fly on a dry airline. On the one end of the spectrum, there are people who will avoid dry airlines at all costs. On the other end of the spectrum, you have billions of people who don’t drink alcohol at all, so this is a non-issue.

Personally I’m probably somewhere in the middle:

Regarding that last point, I do have to give credit to airlines that are dry, but still put effort into their drink selection. Take Saudia, for example, which goes above and beyond in terms of its non-alcoholic drink selection in premium cabins, with everything from cappuccinos and espressos, to excellent mocktails, to non-alcoholic wine.

Airlines often reflect their local customs and religions, so it’s not surprising that many airlines don’t serve alcohol onboard. If you definitely want alcohol when you fly, avoid airlines like Saudia. If you would like a drink when you fly, the good news is that some dry airlines actually let you bring your own alcohol, within certain limitations.

While the above doesn’t cover 100% of situations, hopefully it’s a useful, basic rundown of some of the bigger airlines that are dry.

How strongly do you feel about dry airlines? Does this policy impact your airline choice?

Children’s Place files for bankruptcy protection, seeks buyer

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Children's clothing retailer The Children's Place has filed for chapter 11 bankruptcy protection, it said Sunday.

The company is looking to sell substantially all of its assets and is seeking offers from potential buyers by Dec. 9, with a goal to close a deal by the end of December.

The retailer, whose brands include The Children's Place and Gymboree, says it operates two digital storefronts and 514 North American stores, and has distribution in 13 countries through franchise and wholesale partners. The company's performance has taken a hit in recent years from tariff hikes and slower consumer spending, among other factors.

Its stores and e-commerce sites remain open for now, and the company is planning to continue its ongoing inventory sales with court approval.

"For nearly 60 years, The Children's Place has supported growing families with apparel that customers have come to know and love. We recognize the significance of this development for our employees, customers, vendors, and other partners, and we are committed to conducting an efficient and transparent sale process," said Rudy Morando, co-chief restructuring officer of The Children's Place. "In the meantime, we remain focused on serving the families who have trusted The Children's Place for generations."

This move comes days after interim Children's Place CEO Muhammad Asif Seemab and Chief Strategy Officer Muhammad Umair stepped down from their roles. Seemab and Umair will both remain on the company's board. CFO John Szczepanski took on the role of acting president and principal executive officer.

Seemab is also managing director of Saudi-based Mithaq Capital, which took a majority stake in The Children's Place in 2024 when the company was facing financial trouble.

Even with support from the investors, the company has not been able to significantly improve its financial outlook. In its bankruptcy filing, the company reported about $742 million in assets, compared to about $881 million in debts.

In the second fiscal quarter of 2026, its most recently reported quarter, the company's net sales dropped nearly 19% from the same period the year prior. At the time, Seemab said in a news release that during the quarter the company was "stabilizing our customer file" and "driving traffic to our stores and websites," and saw "lower conversion, combined with a very clearance-heavy mix."

He also pointed to tough comparisons to the prior-year period, due in part to back-to-school shopping demand that "was uniquely elevated ... ahead of anticipated tariff price increases."

The Children's Place stock has plummeted in recent years and was trading around 18 cents per share on Monday.

Chuck Schumer and Hakeem Jeffries Face Democratic Leadership Revolt After Midterms

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The latest conflict brewing in the Democratic Party’s cold war between the blue dogs and the insurgent progressive wing is the fight over its next congressional leaders. But with no alternate candidates stepping up to run, it’s hard to determine how real the threats to replace Senator Chuck Schumer and Representative Hakeem Jeffries actually are.

At least six incoming Democratic senators—including Michigan’s Abdul El-Sayed, South Carolina’s Annie Andrews, Minnesota’s Peggy Flanagan, Illinois’s Juliana Stratton, Kansas’s Adam Hamilton, and Maine’s Troy Jackson—have said they won’t support Schumer for majority leader. Michigan senator Elissa Slotkin has called for a “a new generation of leadership.” Even Cory Booker, who praised Schumer for helping engineer last week’s blue wave, stopped short of saying whether he would support him. On Monday, Richard Blumenthal did the same, but walked by his comments later in day, saying he would support Schumer. And Jon Stewart said that the senator “does not understand the urgency of the moment and what the future demands.” Regardless, Schumer is confident. His aides say he has enough votes to become the next majority leader.

In the House, several incoming Democrats—including Claire Valdez, Angela Gonzalez-Torres, Darializa Avila Chevalier, Melat Kiros, Adam Hamawy, Chris Rabb, and Donavan McKinney—have been noncommittal with their support for Jeffries, while Ohio democrat Don Leonard has threatened to seek alternative leadership unless Jeffries changes his position on Israel.

There’s an online effort to prevent Jeffries from becoming Speaker of the House. A change.org petition demanding that House Democrats reject Jeffries as their next Speaker has attracted more than 1 million signatures as of Sunday, with actor Mark Ruffalo among those encouraging people to sign. But scroll to the bottom of the petition, and you’ll find a disclosure: Its creator, Nicole Whitted, is a paid “Change Ambassador” who receives compensation from change.org in connection with the signatures. “Thousands of organizations and brands worldwide run similar programs and in the social-cause space specifically, it’s a way to encourage people with reach and influence to use their voice for causes they care about,” Whitted told Vanity Fair in a statement.

Whitted also said she was traveling to Washington, DC next week to urge congresspeople to vote against Jeffries, and to meet with Representative Ayanna Pressley, whom Whitted told VF she’ll be supporting for Speaker. Pressley’s team did not respond to Vanity Fair’s request for comment sent on Sunday. Neither did her closest congressional allies, Rashida Tlaib, Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, or Ilhan Omar.

Reports indicate that some congresspeople have been flooded with calls from constituents urging them to find new party leadership. But one Democratic aide told Vanity Fair that they’ve noticed what they claim is a coordinated effort to undermine Jeffries on social media, including AI-generated videos attacking Jeffries that have gotten tens of thousands of engagements.

The Brooklyn congressman does have good relationships with members of both the moderate “blue dogs” and the party’s progressive flank. Ocasio-Cortez has already backed him, and last month, Representative Ro Khanna declared that “Hakeem Jeffries is going to be Speaker.” “Don’t believe what you’re seeing on the internet,” the aide said. “It’s not reality.”

Schumer’s allies, meanwhile, have been reminding skeptics of the work he did to deliver the Democratic majority. The New York senator helped recruit candidates including Roy Cooper and Sherrod Brown, while the Democratic political operation supporting Senate candidates spent lavishly on competitive races. According to final figures provided to Vanity Fair, Senate Majority PAC and its affiliates spent nearly $407 million across races in eight states, including $150 million in Ohio, $67 million apiece in Iowa and Michigan, $65 million in Maine, and $40 million in Texas, all of which flipped. Another $14 million went toward Kansas, with smaller investments in Mississippi and South Carolina—$2.2 million and $750,000 respectively. Some of that money helped elect candidates who are now skeptical about whether Schumer should stay in charge.

In the House, each party will choose its leadership this month, but in order to become Speaker, a candidate still needs to get a majority of the entire chamber, usually 218 members, to vote for them when the new Congress convenes in January. It’s a little easier in the Senate, where the Democrat will only need to win a simple majority of their own caucus in an anonymous early December vote to become majority leader.

El-Sayed has suggested Maryland senator Chris Van Hollen, although a source close to Van Hollen told Vanity Fair that he isn’t planning to run, instead setting his sights on a potential presidential campaign in 2028. “Prior to selecting a majority leader, Senator Van Hollen believes the caucus needs to have an honest and open conversation about how we move an agenda forward that reflects the will of the American people,” says a Democratic aide familiar with the senator’s thinking. “He’s made clear that Democrats need to use their power to immediately end the illegal war with Iran, advance a robust pro-worker kitchen table agenda, and fight political corruption.”

Another name circulating among Democratic aides is Hawaii senator Brian Schatz, a relatively young progressive who has developed relationships across the party’s ideological factions and is already pursuing the number-two leadership position being vacated by retiring senator Dick Durbin.

“He’s the heir apparent,” one Democratic aide told Vanity Fair, describing Schatz as someone who could appeal to both progressives and moderates. Asked about speculation surrounding his leadership ambitions, Schatz’s spokesperson offered a noncommittal response: “I don’t have anything new to share on this but will keep you posted if that changes.”

Some Democrats are also hesitant to replace their experienced leaders after they’ve been given this narrow opportunity to get stuff done with control of both chambers of Congress. “I don’t think you’d want somebody new who’s, like, learning the ropes while we have the opportunity to really get meaningful legislation passed for the American people,” another Democratic aide said.

Still, the fighting online and on cable news exposes a tension within the Democratic Party. Moderates see the blue wave as vindication of the party’s disciplined, affordability-focused strategy. Progressives see it as a mandate for a more ambitious agenda—and a rejection of the establishment’s familiar faces.

Both factions are claiming the election as their own victory. But before they can fight over what to do with their new power, they’ll have to decide who’s going to lead them.

A Sam Altman parody offers an amusing lesson in memory management

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

We verified the RAM numbers are real without sending any emails to OpenAI.

Sam Altman’s recent comments on the Cheeky Pint podcast inspired developer Iain Kay to create a satirical script that allows users to email the OpenAI CEO once per day if more than 10 percent of their system RAM is reported as available. Ram-Altman is described by its creator as a “parody project” launched in response to Altman’s viral remarks about RAM in your personal PC.

As reported by The Verge, on the October 29 episode recorded with Stripe President John Collison, Altman said, “A lot of people want local inference. And somehow, they assume that the problem is solved now because we are going to have open source models, we’re going to be able to run them on our computers, and so we’re not going to need all the stuff in the data center. But from a global allocation perspective, actually, it’s quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world’s limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer and being used 5 percent of the time.”

Altman has since explained that his comments were taken out of context and briefly changed his display name to “RAM Altman” on X, which Kay calls on the project GitHub, “objectively excellent and where the project gets its name.”

In an email to Ars, Kay said, “Ram-Altman is a bash script that takes MemTotal minus MemAvailable from /proc/meminfo, so the figure includes page cache and reclaimable slab but leaves out tmpfs and shared memory.”

If the script only showed what Linux reports as “free” RAM, it would paint an incomplete picture. Linux often reports a surprisingly small amount of “free” RAM, as it doesn’t count memory that is currently claimable for use. Linux will fill available RAM caches with file data for quick access, but the kernel can reclaim that usage at a moment’s notice when needed. The MemAvailable figure is the kernel’s own estimate of how much memory it could hand over without swapping.

Users can schedule the script to ask the kernel for a status update every five minutes, and if more than 10 percent of RAM is available, it triggers a joke email from one of several joke templates. The script limits outgoing emails to one per day.

The GitHub page for the RAM-Altman project includes a dry-run option that any Linux user with a Bash terminal should be able to paste and run without the need for email trolling. When the threshold is exceeded, each dry run output features one generated joke email, so repeated runs produce different amusing examples.

A test run of the script on my Fedora 44 KDE machine confirmed the author’s measurements. Inside the System – Info Center of my Fedora desktop, the system reports 14.9 GiB of total usable system RAM after the kernel and firmware take their needed slices of the 16 GiB of physical memory. This reflects the total amount of capacity the system has to work with, not the amount available at any given time. The Ram-Altman script shows that of that 14.9 GiB total pool, 10 GiB is used, with 4.9 GiB available (33.1 percent). That figure isn’t the simple sum of the “free” and “cache” lines that the script displays, but a kernel-calculated figure that accounts for the full amount of memory that can be reclaimed.

The project concedes the nuance that Altman was referring to how much time memory spends being utilized, while the script measures how much capacity is free at the time of measurement. While the script might tell Altman that 33 percent of your system RAM is available, it doesn’t outline how often that’s the case. The concession steps on the joke slightly, but it keeps everyone honest.

The GitHub is wise to point out that “--dry-run is the best way to enjoy this. It costs nothing and all the jokes are there.” Used irresponsibly, this cheeky joke could morph into a harassment tool, and Kay quickly points out that the script does not include anyone’s email, including Altman’s. Users who deploy their own operating version are encouraged to only use their own real email addresses and honor any requests to stop, as is outlined at the bottom of the script-generated output.

If you’re interested in taking a look at some of the emails generated without having to spin up a Linux machine that reports the system status every five minutes, the ram-altman.lol project website rotates through sample emails generated by the script. Kay writes on the website that, “In fairness, he says the remarks were taken out of context, posted ‘i want you to have so much ram’, and briefly went by Ram Altman on X. That makes this script a daily progress report.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

PepsiCo to cut about 1,000 jobs as North American business struggles

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

PepsiCo is cutting about 1,000 jobs across North America as the company tries to turn around lagging sales in its home market.

"We have a clear ambition for PepsiCo North America: to build a stronger, more future-fit business that delivers industry-leading growth," Ram Krishnan, CEO of PepsiCo North America, wrote in a Monday letter to employees obtained by CNBC. "To do that, we need to be faster, more connected, and more capable, while continuing to invest in growth — behind our people, capabilities and brands."

Over the next week, Pepsi plans to cut jobs as it says it aims to streamline its operations and better connect teams across the company's North American business. PepsiCo employs about 306,000 people worldwide, including roughly 125,000 in the United States, as of Dec. 27, according to a company filing.

The Wall Street Journal first reported the coming layoffs.

In October, PepsiCo cut its full-year earnings outlook, citing the struggles in its home market. During the third quarter, its North American beverage unit saw volume shrink 2%, while its North American food division reported flat volume.

While the company has tried to boost sales by cutting prices and investing in marketing, the turnaround is taking longer than expected, Chief Financial Officer Steve Schmitt said on the company's earnings conference call.

Shares of Pepsi rose slightly in afternoon trading. The stock is down nearly 1% this year, lagging behind the S&P 500's gains of nearly 11% over the same period.

Supreme Court allows Trump administration to continue border wall construction

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The Supreme Court on Monday cleared the way for the Trump administration to resume construction of a border wall in the Big Bend region of southwestern Texas. In a four-page, unsigned opinion, the justices granted the government’s request to pause an order by a federal district judge that had temporarily halted the project. Only Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson indicated, without explanation, that she would have denied the government’s request.

The dispute began earlier this year, when then-Homeland Security Secretary Kristi Noem and her successor, Markwayne Mullin, issued three waivers of various environmental and conservation laws to authorize construction of the wall in the Big Bend region, which includes parts of Big Bend National Park. Noem and Mullin relied on a provision of the Illegal Immigration Reform and Immigrant Responsibility Act of 1996 that gives the DHS secretary the power to “waive all legal requirements” necessary “to ensure expeditious construction” of border walls and roads. When they announced the waivers, Noem and Mullin indicated that the Big Bend area “is an area of high illegal entry” requiring additional walls and barriers.

A group of plaintiffs opposed to the construction – an environmental group, a local landowner who works as a river guide, and a group formed to preserve a church – went to federal court to challenge the waivers’ legality. On Oct. 2, U.S. District Judge Kathleen Cardone temporarily barred the Department of Homeland Security from continuing construction on the border wall in the Big Bend region “until and unless” the government complies with environmental and other applicable laws.

In a written opinion that she released on Oct. 14, Cardone reasoned that the challengers were likely to prevail on their claim that the DHS secretary did not have the authority to issue the waivers. She relied on the major questions doctrine – the idea that if Congress intends to give a federal agency the power to make decisions of vast economic or political significance, it must say so very clearly. In particular, she emphasized, the Big Bend region is not an area with a high number of illegal entries. To the contrary, she stated, although it “comprises over 25% of the U.S.-Mexico border,” it makes up only a tiny percentage (“between 0.3% and 2.2%”) of the migrants apprehended while crossing the border. Therefore, she wrote, “if the Big Bend Sector is an area of high illegal entry, so is every other sector, and the waiver authority would apply to the entire U.S.-Mexico border. If that were the case, Congress could have simply authorized construction ‘to deter illegal crossings.’” The major questions doctrine instructs courts to avoid “interpretations that would render limiting language meaningless,” Cardone concluded.

After Cardone declined to pause her ruling to give the government time to seek Supreme Court review, the Trump administration came to the Supreme Court, where it asked the justices not only to temporarily freeze Cardone’s order but also to grant review and summarily (that is, without additional briefing or oral argument) reverse it. U.S. Solicitor General D. John Sauer argued that Cardone lacked the power to consider the challengers’ claim based on the major questions doctrine because their complaint specifically alleged that the DHS secretary “lacked ‘clear congressional authorization’ … to issue the waivers” under the IIRIRA – which, he said, was a statutory claim that “Congress denied district courts” the authority “to entertain.” But in any event, Sauer continued, even if the DHS secretary’s power to issue waivers under IIRIRA “implicates a question of ‘vast economic and political significance,’ Congress did clearly authorize the Secretary both to build border barriers and to waive legal requirements in order to facilitate their expeditious construction.”

The challengers urged the justices to leave Cardone’s order in place. Eric Glitzenstein, a lawyer representing the plaintiffs, told the justices in a 40-page filing that if her order were paused or lifted, it would permit the Trump administration to “immediately resume blasting, bulldozing, road construction, and other border barrier-related construction across one of the most remote and ecologically and culturally significant landscapes in the United States.”

The challengers argued that the major questions doctrine applied to their claim. They emphasized that the DHS secretary’s “decision to waive myriad federal, state, local, and tribal laws across vast geography for the purpose of building border infrastructure, even through national and state parks where border crossings are miniscule, is a truly ‘extraordinary’ assertion of power.” Because Congress “did not clearly authorize the sweeping exercise of power asserted here,” they wrote, the government’s “actions therefore amount to a constitutional violation of the major questions doctrine.”

In Monday’s opinion, the justices denied the government’s request to summarily reverse Cardone’s ruling but granted its request to put it on hold. “The Government,” the court explained, “is exceedingly likely to prevail on its argument” that Cardone did not have the power to consider the challengers’ “so-called major-questions-doctrine claim.” That claim, the court emphasized, contends only “that the Secretary exceeded his statutory authority,” which is not a constitutional claim. This is true, the court continued, even if the challengers rely on the major-questions doctrine, because that doctrine is a rule used to interpret laws. “There is no such thing as a ‘constitutional’ major-questions claim.”

The other factors that courts normally consider in deciding whether to pause a lower court’s decision also point in the government’s favor, the justices said. For example, Cardone’s order “delays the Government’s construction of border barriers and roads in the Big Bend Sector” and, as a result, “impedes the Government from advancing its ‘compelling interests’ in border security and integrity.” Moreover, the government is suffering permanent harm from the costs – “nearly half a million dollars … per day” – that it is incurring while the construction is on hold, but which cannot be recovered. Finally, the court wrote, “[t]he Government’s interest … outweighs any aesthetic, recreational, and environmental harm alleged by” the challengers.

Amy Howe is the co-founder of SCOTUSblog and its primary reporter.

11 Classic Italian Dishes That Belong On Your Thanksgiving Table

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Italian food is inherently celebratory. The cuisine embodies togetherness, featuring giant loaves of semolina bread, big salads, and overflowing family-style platters that snake their way around the table until there's a little bit of everything on everyone's plate. And while we didn't all necessarily grow up eating meatballs, gnocchi, and lasagna, these meals have slowly become a fundamental part of the American cooking canon. Italian food isn't necessarily easy to prepare, but its familiarity and simple elegance make the dishes feel accessible, even if they're difficult to master.

Italian food is also so well suited for the holiday table because of its adaptability. Regardless of your culinary background or self-identified food heritage, most classic Italian dishes can be reconfigured to fit any cuisine or occasion, including Thanksgiving. When you think about it, the two are a natural fit. Italian food is hearty, cozy, and comforting, with plenty of creamy carbohydrates and hearty main courses. The same goes for your typical Thanksgiving fare, allowing this list to feature recipe ideas that blend the two. Think pumpkin-stuffed ravioli, turkey lasagna, and risotto topped with roasted butternut squash. These interesting mashups will undoubtedly bring some extra excitement (and cheese) to your upcoming holiday menu.

A whole roast turkey is one of the most divisive holiday dishes. Done correctly, it's succulent, juicy, and full of bold poultry flavor; prepared improperly, you're left with pounds of bland, boring meat. But you don't need to cook a 10-pound bird to get turkey on the Thanksgiving table. These slow-cooker Italian turkey and zucchini meatballs use classic Italian cooking methods and ingredients to bring Thanksgiving flair to the holiday table. Meatballs are typically viewed as casual fare, but with the right cooking techniques and seasonings, they can be just as delicious as anything.

This recipe calls for a mixture of ground turkey meat and shredded zucchini. Why? The zucchini helps the meatballs retain moisture and adds some savory vegetal notes to the meat. These two ingredients are paired with a supporting cast of garlic, egg, breadcrumbs, and Pecorino Romano cheese to round out the flavor and give the meatballs a firmer texture. More importantly, the slow cooking keeps the meatballs submerged in a homemade tomato sauce, trapping moisture and keeping that notoriously dry turkey meat nice and luscious. This technique not only makes perfectly prepared meatballs, but also lets you tend to Grandma and Cousin Peter without worrying about something else overcooking.

This rich turkey tomato Bolognese is another creative way to add turkey to your Thanksgiving feast without cooking, carving, and roasting a massive piece of poultry. Bolognese is essentially a slow-simmered Italian sauce made with tomatoes and ground meat. As it cooks, the ingredients meld together, creating a thick, stew-like consistency that's greater than the sum of its parts. Although it is typically made with ground beef or pork, you can easily substitute turkey without losing any of the dish's heartiness or texture. The aromatics give it a sweet undertone, and the white wine helps deglaze the pan, capturing those brown bits of concentrated flavor.

But what sets this recipe apart is another ingredient synonymous with an authentic Bolognese yet largely omitted in jarred varieties: milk. Dairy may seem like a strange addition, but it gives the sauce a smooth, rich texture and helps tenderize the meat. Equally important to building your Bolognese sauce is pairing it with the correct pasta. Spaghetti is the most common but not the best option (at least according to Ina Garten). Instead, choose something flatter with more surface area, like tagliatelle or pappardelle. If you're feeling tubular, go with a shape with a wider diameter, like rigatoni, so the sauce can get trapped inside.

Sausage has rightfully earned its place on the Thanksgiving table, so much so that you can find it in everything from stuffing to cornbread casseroles to white gravy. But depending on which style you choose, the meat can easily overpower or oversalt a dish. When preparing this zucchini involtini, it's best to use an uncooked, Italian-style sausage. Unlike those popular, pre-cooked, vacuum-sealed chicken sausages, a quality Italian sausage comes raw and in a natural casing; otherwise, you can't sufficiently break up, brown, and mix the meat with other ingredients.

This appetizer requires some preparation, but the final result is well worth the effort. The most laborious task is slicing the zucchini into long, thin planks and piping them full of the sausage and ricotta filling. After you prepare the rolls, the rest of the dish comes together quickly. Just top each one with shredded cheese, and bake them together in a thin layer of sauce. The chew of the sausage paired with the sweet tomato sauce and creamy ricotta makes for an unforgettable appetizer that tastes just as impressive as it looks. If you don't want to make your tomato sauce from scratch, you can easily substitute your favorite jarred marinara.

Agnolotti started in the Piedmont region of northern Italy as a humble, pinched pasta that farmers would make to use up leftover bits of meat. Unlike ravioli, which are perfectly square, agnolotti are thin and rectangular, more like a standard bedroom pillow rather than a throw. This agnolotti di zucca is the perfect seasonal pasta, especially if you're in the mood to make fresh dough.

And while many Thanksgiving centerpieces feature a cornucopia of squash and gourds, this recipe actually includes them in the dish, specifically in the agnolotti filling, which is made with a mixture of ricotta cheese and roasted delicata squash. If you don't want to deal with chopping and de-seeding, a pumpkin purée will also work, but the flavor may fall short without those natural caramel notes you get from freshly roasted squash. The fresh pasta is an excellent foil, the dough chewy and toothsome as it cradles the delicate filling. After the agnolotti are stuffed and boiled, they're finished in a pan with butter and sage for a bite that's all things autumnal. Obviously, this dish isn't exactly light fare, so serve it in place of your more classic holiday carbohydrates.

Speaking of ravioli, you can't have a list of classic Italian dishes without mentioning this instantly recognizable stuffed pasta. Moving beyond squash, this homemade ravioli recipe focuses on the other orange gourd: pumpkin. But we're not talking about the canned stuff; this dish gets its unique flavor from freshly roasted sugar pumpkin. Does it require more prep work? Yes. You have to remove the seeds and stringy pulp, roast the pumpkin, and scoop out the flesh, but these extra steps add a bright earthiness you just can't recreate with canned pumpkin.

Unlike most pasta fillings, which are salty, savory, and creamy, this recipe goes in another direction, pairing pulverized almonds with ground nutmeg, ricotta cheese, and, of course, roasted pumpkin. The finished ravioli are coated in a traditional butter-sage sauce, furthering that autumn feel. Again, these require homemade dough, but it's fairly forgiving if you roll it out to the proper thickness — slightly less than 1 millimeter thick. Cut the dough in circles, and fold over the filling to make pretty little half moons, a nice, festive homage to the fall harvest.

The Thanksgiving table is usually covered with the classics: stuffing, cornbread, turkey, and sweet potato casserole. But this predictability can easily veer into monotony. Change is difficult, but it can be a good thing, especially for your palate. Reinventing your holiday menu isn't so much about eliminating as it is about balancing. Holiday food is notoriously rich and sweet; adding a dish that's salty, spicy, and earthy can help bring some relief.

These spicy sausage stuffed mushrooms deliver all of the above. A stuffed mushroom is a festive appetizer, but if you use super-sized portobellos, they quickly become worthy of a main course. The stuffing is a mixture of spicy sausage, breadcrumbs, egg, and grated cheese. If you want to sacrifice a mushroom (or stems), you can dice it up and add it in there too. Once the filling is sautéed, all you need to do is clean and stuff your mushrooms, then bake until everything is cooked through. Compared to making and filling your own fresh pasta dough, this appetizer is pretty easy to throw together. You can also prepare these ahead of time and just bake them off when ready. The sausage's pepperiness, the crunch of the breadcrumbs, and the mushrooms' earthy, umami notes make this a definite crowd-pleaser.

Risotto is cultural fusion at its finest, blending northern Italian cooking traditions with the culinary influence of Middle Eastern traders and travelers. It's a simple dish that requires only a modest collection of ingredients. But don't be fooled by its understated appearance — risotto takes a lot of skill and patience to execute successfully. If you've ever tasted a quality risotto, you'll know its texture is what makes it so elegant and luxurious. As the Arborio rice cooks, the grains release their natural starches, creating a smooth, velvety texture without any cream.

This butternut squash and sage risotto follows the same basic principles, but its flavor profile leans more toward seasonal fall ingredients and isn't quite what you would find at a classic Italian restaurant. The butternut squash is diced into cubes, tossed with honey and olive oil, roasted, and served on top of the finished dish. But more important than the garnish is the cooking of the risotto itself. The Arborio rice should be lightly sautéed with oil and aromatics until opaque before receiving its first ladle of broth. From there, you're going one cup at a time, waiting for the grains to absorb the broth or stock before adding the next round of liquid. The risotto is ready when it naturally spreads across the plate.

Turkey is the iconic Thanksgiving centerpiece, but what really gets the whole family gathered around the table is the variety of smooth and scoopable side dishes. Nobody is surprised by mashed potatoes, macaroni and cheese, and corn pudding, but if you want something with more Italian flair and a nuttier, toastier finish, consider adding polenta to your menu.

Polenta is essentially a cornmeal porridge that's mounted with butter. It's rich and filling and probably the reason Uncle Bob will fall asleep before dessert. This polenta pasticciata (also known as "messed up" polenta) is like a mix of scalloped potatoes and a cheesy casserole. To make the dish, you cook, cool, and refrigerate the polenta in a baking dish until set. You then slice the polenta into half-inch-thick slices and stack them like a lasagna, adding alternating layers of polenta and cheese. This recipe also uses a trifle of fromage, including Gruyère, Gorgonzola dolce (an Italian blue cheese), and rich, creamy mascarpone. Finally, you bake the whole thing until golden brown and bubbly. Be forewarned: You may never be able to go back to eating an ordinary casserole ever again.

You can skip the turkey; you can ixnay the green bean casserole; you can even pass on the sweet potatoes, but it's hard to call it a Thanksgiving feast without stuffing. I never understood why we relegate this savory bread pudding to just one day of the year, as it's easy to put together, uniquely herby, and a great way to salvage that stale loaf. Given these characteristics, you can take your stuffing in hundreds of directions. But since this article focuses on classic Italian dishes, we're sticking with that cadre of ingredients.

At first glance, this sausage and mushroom stuffing seems relatively ordinary; it doesn't use a scoop of gochujang or soak its bread in a dashi broth. But if you look closer, you'll see it's special in its simplicity. It all starts with the bread, which is a nice, tart sourdough. Next is the spicy, porky, and piquant Italian sausage. Finally, it's the broth. Sure, you can use chicken stock, but this recipe calls for pouring your vermouth-infused broth back into the pan with the cooked fennel, onion, and celery to infuse it with that deep, naturally sweet vegetable flavor. Right before baking, you quickly cook your mushrooms and add them to the bread mixture with a sprinkling of fresh herbs. No superfluous additions here; each ingredient has a place and a purpose, which is why this makes for such a terrific stuffing.

It's hard to pinpoint exactly when macaroni and cheese made its way to the Thanksgiving table, but this classic comfort food is now somewhat of a holiday fixture. And while it's hard to find fault with any bubbling crock of cheesy noodles, if you want to deviate from this French-inspired dish and go Italian, make this turkey and spinach gnocchi Alfredo bake.

Rarely does one recipe have it all, but this one comes pretty close. The ingredient list could almost be healthy — if you closed your eyes before pouring in the cup of heavy cream. But it's not all richness upon richness. The foundation of this dish is diced — not ground — turkey breast, gnocchi, spinach, and cheese. Still, you'll find it surprisingly balanced, thanks to the turkey's meatiness and slightly gamey flavor. The sauce is made with turkey or chicken stock thickened with roux and finished with cream. After dousing the gnocchi in the sauce, topping with mozzarella, and baking in the oven, you'll have a show-stopping side dish that will likely upstage anything else on the table.

No matter the occasion — dinner party, sweet 16 celebration, competitive evening of Catan — a baking dish filled to the brim with bubbling waves of saucy pasta sheets and melted cheese always turns heads. Lasagna is that dish, always here to nourish and satisfy. A big tray can feed an army (of at least six) comfortably, all without the effort of preparing individual burgers, cutlets, steaks, or filets.

Traditional lasagna, or at least the one most of us know today, is an import from Southern Italian immigrants, made primarily with a simple meat ragu, ricotta cheese, and mozzarella (and pasta sheets, of course). This spinach turkey lasagna isn't much different, the main change being substituting ground turkey for ground beef. The ground turkey adds some extra chew and texture to the somewhat mushy lasagna. Using a leaner meat also helps cut down on the dish's overall richness and allows other flavors, like the spinach and marinara sauce, to shine. But most importantly, it's the fact that it's turkey that makes this minimal-fuss main course such a cool and tasty culinary crossover.

Best Actress Frontrunner Julianne Moore Nailed ‘The Debut’ by Singing, Styling Her Hair Like Liza Minnelli, and Avoiding Playing ‘Some Suffering Mom’

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

“Hello, I’m Julie,” said a radiant Julianne Moore, holding out her hand as she popped out of the elevator at Middleburg, Virginia’s Salamander Resort. Her hair was red, her skin was white, her dress as yellow as gold. A few hours later after a rapturous Middleburg Film Festival reaction to “The Debut” (December 11), we talked in a hotel meeting room. The A24 crowdpleaser has been wowing audiences and critics since Telluride.

Jesse Eisenberg has done it again. His follow-up to “A Real Pain,” which won Kieran Culkin the Supporting Actor Oscar, could score Best Actress for Moore. The musical comedy has earned Moore raves on the festival circuit for her role as a sad suburban homemaker turned anxious aspiring actress in a New Jersey community theater. A sixth Oscar nomination is inevitable, and possibly a second Oscar win after “Still Alice” in 2015. It’s hard to believe she launched her career in 1988 as a regular on soap opera “As the World Turns.” Related Stories Rejected Twice by Sundance, ‘Mouse’ Is One of the Year’s Best Movies. Why Aren’t More People Talking About It? From Los Javis to Lord and Miller, Director Duos Are a High-Risk, High-Reward Prospect in the Oscar Race

Moore is the current Best Actress frontrunner, even though she has won before. Sometimes nabbing an Oscar is a disadvantage in the Oscar race. But not if the performance is undeniable: Katharine Hepburn holds the record with four, while nipping at her heels are three-time winners Meryl Streep, Frances McDormand, Jack Nicholson, and Sean Penn.

Eisenberg wrote a rich character for Moore, knowing what the veteran actress could do with the childlike housewife Mona who, in order to learn how to act, must find herself. The director and actress had worked together on “When You Finish Saving the World” (2022). “[‘The Debut‘] is about this woman who has been chewed up but not spit out,” said Eisenberg, quoting Mona’s signature song. “It’s my point of view through somebody I consider a muse. I was writing something about myself as a seven-year-old in 1990, but I couldn’t picture anybody else except her doing it.”

It helped that Moore had told Eisenberg exactly what she was looking for. “I said I had just done ‘Dear Evan Hansen,’ and I loved singing, and I would love to have another opportunity,” she said. “I told him that I would love to do more comedy, and I didn’t want to play some suffering mom.”

Eisenberg put all of this in the screenplay, “but in the most surprising and inventive way,” she said. “He’s such a specific writer. He’s so incredibly empathetic. He has a compassionate worldview. He understands creativity. All of that stuff went into this wonderful and delightful story. It’s joyful and life-affirming too.”

Moore didn’t know she was playing a seven-year-old version of Eisenberg until he started telling that to the press. “Then he was like, ‘This is my experience of being a kid and going to community theater.’ But I could sense that there was a personal point of view in it. I very much felt with Mona that she was on a subjective journey, and that he was inside of that. With Mona, I always knew he was looking through her.”

“The Debut” is told from her point of view, as she tries to talk the overbearing local community theater director Jerry (Paul Giamatti) into hiring her, an untrained actress. He does it because he has no one else. But first, he must teach her how to act.

Mona dresses like a schoolgirl, with skirts, blouses, cardigan sweaters. “She’s so contained, and the way she learns is so literal,” said Moore, “the way a baby learns, like, you make a face at a baby, the baby makes a face back, but they don’t have intention behind it. But they’re learning things in an exterior way, and then they match it with feeling later on. So everything she’s doing is a childlike way of learning.”

Mona goes from an invisible nonentity in her own home to someone who demands attention. “She’s a kid who has found friends for the first time,” said Eisenberg. “She’s the girl who transferred to the right school at seven years old, and then the first rehearsal happens, and she is told she is terrible, and she has no idea why. And it’s the girl who transferred to the right school and gets an F on the very first test, and then the rest of the semester is panicking, and all she’s seeing is everybody else get A’s, and she still has the F, and she doesn’t know what to do. And then the teacher pulls her aside and says, ‘You either show up, or you are out on your ass.’ And something switches in her, and she makes the decision: ‘I am going to show up.’ And she begins to turn into a monster.”

The theater troupe embraces Mona as she unfolds like a flower. “We’ve been like those theater kids cracking corny jokes and finding community,” said Moore. “It’s all done with so much affection. Jesse looks at that world with not a hint of malice.”

Giamatti’s director relentlessly pushes the newbie through her paces until they have a powerful confrontation on the empty stage where he forces her to break down. Eisenberg reduced the actors’ anxiety by placing the eight-page showstopper in the middle of the shoot: not the beginning, and not the end. On the first day he went over to Moore’s apartment to talk about the movie. He said, “I really don’t want you to worry about that scene. I don’t want you to worry about memorizing that because I know that as an actor, I would I lose my mind. I would just be drilling it all the time, and I wouldn’t be able to concentrate. We’re going to have a teleprompter on that day. So don’t worry about it.”

So Moore worked on it normally, she said, “like I worked on everything else in the movie. So when we got to it, I knew it. I didn’t need a teleprompter. He knew instinctively as a director and an actor that what scares us sometimes is just a volume of language. He didn’t want to freeze me up. And having a partner like Paul who was so awake and aware and generous made it a pleasure. We were enjoying it when we did shoot it.”

Moore and Giamatti had never worked together before. She once introduced herself to him in a bookstore in Montauk. “He was very shy,” she said. “He is the greatest scene partner. I didn’t expect him to be so generous. He’s so funny, and he’s so dear. He’s present, incredibly modest, and as a partner, so available. Paul and I would work on it occasionally. When we were doing that scene, the rhythm was so important in it. You throw something, he hits it right back. It was our sense together of keeping it aloft, keeping it moving, keeping the river flowing. It felt electric with the two of us.”

The big blowout happens early in the story. “In a lot of movies, that kind of set piece would happen later,” said Moore. “Instead, it’s like a discovery place. It’s where she starts to begin to unlock herself emotionally, but she doesn’t learn from it. She comes in the next day, and starts to rehearse. And her belief is that acting is size, that it’s just huge, right? That’s when she starts yelling. She thinks it’s about being loud, which then allows her to go on the next journey with Jerry, where he says, ‘Let’s talk about feeling and reality and intention.’ So she’s slowly putting these pieces together. But that’s the first moment where she attaches something outside of herself to her own emotion.”

The whole shoot was fast, as Eisenberg didn’t have time to muck about and often shot just one take of a scene. “She and Paul Giamatti are so good on take one,” said Eisenberg. “It’s embarrassing. This is a small budget movie. The first two days of our schedule, we filmed in one day. The first two days, which were big ensemble musical numbers, we filmed in one day because we were prepared as a crew, but the actors were perfect on take one.”

Moore didn’t feel rushed. “It doesn’t feel, working with Jesse, like you’re moving too fast,” she said. “Working in independent film, you’re always moving quickly because you need to. You don’t have the time. You don’t have the money. So you want to move through things quickly. But I never felt rushed with him. I never I never felt like I was leaving something, that I didn’t get to complete something.”

The challenge for Moore, as it is for every actor shooting out of order, was making her character changes seamless. Eisenberg started with the theater scenes, then shot her family, and then jumped into Mona’s increasing weirdness. “The hardest thing always is making sure that you’ve pitched things correctly,” Moore said. “You want to make sure that you’ve managed to match it to another scene. So it was always hard to figure out. That’s our job.”

Eisenberg wanted Mona to move from looking quite childlike in the beginning, and swiftly move through her evolving transformations. Moore had fun with the costumes, figuring out the crazy wigs, makeup, and outfits in the second half. “Jesse had written that Mona electrocutes herself and destroys her hair, and that she was going to get her hair cut,” said Moore, “but he hadn’t written in a color change. We were messing around with it and trying to figure out what to do with hair and makeup and wardrobe and we had this idea that by having her dye her hair dark and chop it off, I wanted to homage the great ladies of musical theater, Liza Minnelli and Chita Rivera and Patti LuPone. It’s a wonderful Easter egg for that character.”

Making everything easier for Moore was actor-turned-director Eisenberg, who’d tell her when to ‘raise the stakes’ or ‘go hotter.’ “He’s maybe the most collaborative person I’ve ever worked with my entire life,” said Moore. “He listens to everyone. He asks people for opinions. He’ll ask people for notes, in a way that I would be like, ‘you don’t have to do that. This is your movie. It’s your creation.’ But he does collate opinion in a way. He’s fascinating in his generosity.”

Needless to say, singing her centerpiece song (“This City May Have Chewed Me Up (But It Hasn’t Spit Me Out”) was nervous-making, given that Moore was surrounded by Broadway super-belters like Tony winners Danny Burstein and Bonnie Tillman. “These people have the voices of angels,” said Moore. “When I did sing that song for the first time, and they’re all sitting there watching me, I think they did feel worried for me because they have so much ease. The song that she sings is so beautiful. In the singing of the song, she’s attaching her own emotional experience suddenly to the song. She understands the song in the moment she’s singing that. Somehow she wants more, and she knows that this is an avenue for her to find that. And so it’s a journey back to herself: finding that creativity and connection and community brings her to an essential sense of self.”

If degree of difficulty counts with Oscar voters, Moore may have that advantage.

BMW Sets 2 New Nürburgring Records With Help from Pirelli

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A revised semi-slick tire helped the carmaker knock down the compact car and touring car lap records at the Nordschleife.

Pirelli’s P Zero Trofeo RS is one of its most extreme performance tires, one capable of helping performance cars extract the most out of their potential. And that’s especially true when the company works with automakers directly—as the Italian rubbermaker did with BMW, developed a bespoke version specifically for the BMW M range. The new semi-slick tire remains street legal, but doesn’t sacrifice at-the-limit grip. And it helped BMW set two new ‘Ring lap records with its BMW M2 with the M Performance Track Kit and BMW M3 CS Touring.

The BMW M2 with the M Performance Track Kit managed a lap time of 7:25.068minutes. This lowered the Nürburgring Nordschleife compact car record, previously held by the M2 CS, by 0.5 seconds. The long-roof BMW M3, however, made more significant history: BMW’s M3 CS Touring lapped the 12.9-mile racetrack in 7:29.49 minutes, setting a new record for the fastest touring car ever by shaving off more than five seconds. The previous touring car lap record of 7:35.06 was held by the standard BMW M3 Touring. A combination of increased horsepower, stronger brakes, and custom rubber—as well as BMW M Development engineer Jörg Weidinger behind the wheel—pushed the wagon to success.

“The bespoke development program is the result of close collaboration between Pirelli and BMW M’s R&D teams. Thanks to advanced virtual development techniques, the two teams were able to adapt the tire’s characteristics to the different requirements from the earliest stages of the project, in line with Pirelli’s Perfect Fit approach, which aims to create products tailored to each specific model or application,” the tire maker said in a statement.

Pirelli initially tested the tires alongside BMW at the Nürburgring Nordschleife, and the tires were also later tested at the Nardò test circuit in Italy. BMW and Pirelli have collaborated on over 30 bespoke tire projects across the P Zero family over the years, and like many others, a keen eye can tell which ones are made for Bimmers; the manufacturer-specific tires feature a BMW star marking, as a nod to the integrated development process.

A New York transplant hailing from the Pacific Northwest, Emmet White has a passion for anything that goes: cars, bicycles, planes, and motorcycles. After learning to ride at 17, Emmet worked in the motorcycle industry before joining Autoweek in 2022 and Road & Track in 2024. The woes of alternate side parking have kept his fleet moderate, with a 2014 Volkswagen Jetta GLI and a BMW 318i E30 street parked in his Queens community.

Is Sexy Art Back, or Is It Just Me?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

From Willem de Kooning’s miserable nudes to Alexandra Barth’s hot and bothered architecture, sex is in the air in Chicago.

CHICAGO — I see a lot of exhibitions in Chicago, and lately, I have been noticing more than a few that feel almost ridiculously erotic. I mean, I get it, there's always been plenty of eros in art, but I haven't observed it much lately at the school where I teach nor in the shows that I view. Is sex back? Or it could just be me. I turned 50 this past year, and popular culture suggests that this might be what happens around my age, à la Sharon Horgan's Youth and Olivia Colman's Wicker, both recent screen-based explorations of lusty midlife ladyhood.

For those who have never had one or maybe never will, female middle-age libido sets at either zero or 1,000, on a scale of one to 100. It is not an easy ride. Shifting from one state to the other causes severe whiplash for driver and passengers alike. That’s based on an admittedly minuscule survey, though fear not, this will not be a personal essay about my own sexuality or that of my acquaintances. God, no. Maybe after everyone’s dead. What proceeds, instead, is a round-up of exhibitions that, for me, have a strong connecting thread, obviously red, and not discussed much if at all in the various associated didactics.

What shows? Last spring, Alice Tippit’s sly, meticulous paintings at the DePaul Art Museum had me thinking I probably needed to start talking to a therapist again, because what was going on in my head was not articulated anywhere in the otherwise delightful catalogue. Not even close. During the summer, SHADOW at tête-à-tête-à-tête, a wicked little curatorial project of Shannon Stratton, included Holly Murkerson’s sensuous photographs of a stone passageway worth entering and two dark leather-and-wood stools by David Sprecher and Neiva Mulhearn that required straddling and peering into.

What wasn’t a turn-on was the Art Institute of Chicago’s summer blockbuster, Willem de Kooning Drawing. You wouldn’t know from the glowing reviews or polite museum text that miserable naked women filled the walls. I like a good nude as much as anyone, but at some point, maybe it was the third room — definitely by the time I got to the giant rowboat charcoals of the mid-60s — de Kooning’s subjects started to look like crime victims.

Left: Lee Miller, “Woman with Hand on Head” (also known as “Coiffure”) (1931), photograph; right: Lee Miller, “Charlie Chaplin with chandelier" (1932), photograph (both photos courtesy Art Institute of Chicago)

Arsenal still tracking long-term striker target, door still open

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Graeme Hanna is the site lead of Read Rangers and also writes about West Ham for Read West Ham. A Rangers season-ticket holder and freelance writer, he has previously written for Vital Rangers, Rangers Review, Football Ground Guide and Give Me Sport, and has a long association with the Follow Follow fanzine.

Arsenal are still monitoring Bayer Leverkusen striker Christian Kofane as they weigh up options to strengthen their attack, according to BBC Sport.

The Gunners already know the 20-year-old well.

Kofane featured for Leverkusen when the sides met in last season’s Champions League round of 16, when Mikel Arteta’s team progressed 3-1 on aggregate.

The renewed interest follows heavy speculation during the summer.

In April, Kofane’s agent Eric Depolo said Arsenal’s interest was genuine and confirmed he was in contact with the club, claiming Kofane would give them a top striker for the next decade.

Leverkusen were reported to value him at around €70m, though Depolo had talked up a €100m figure. The Gunners were said to be ‘in pole position’, but no move materialised.

Arsenal wanted a forward in the last window but missed out on Vinicius Jr and Julian Alvarez, while Morgan Rogers joined Chelsea instead.

Kofane’s rise has been rapid. He moved from AS Nylon in Cameroon to Albacete in 2024, scoring eight goals in 20 Segunda Division games, before joining Leverkusen in July 2025.

He made 44 appearances in his first season in Germany, including 11 in the Champions League, and has three goals in six games this term. The frontman is currently sidelined with a hamstring injury.

If and when, the striker leaves North Rhine-Westphalia, Arsenal face competition.

Manchester United, Real Madrid and Bayern Munich are all reported to be tracking him, amid reports his contract talks with Leverkusen have stalled.

The striker situation at the Emirates adds context. Viktor Gyokeres scored 21 goals last season but has been second choice behind Kai Havertz and has started only once in the league.

A January deal looks unlikely unless Arsenal sell one of their current forwards first.

For now, Kofane remains a long-term target rather than an imminent signing, but Arsenal’s interest has clearly not faded.

SpaceX goes for a Starship double catch

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

On Monday, Nov. 9, SpaceX is targeting 10:03 p.m. EST to launch USSF-627 on a Falcon 9 from Space Launch Complex 4 East at Vandenberg Space Force Base in California. The mission is a classified payload for the U.S. Space Force. The booster will aim for the droneship Of Course I Still Love You in the Pacific Ocean.

On Wednesday, Nov. 11, the state-owned China Aerospace Science and Technology Corporation (CASC) is targeting 6:40 a.m. EST for a Long March 2D launch from the Jiuquan Satellite Launch Center in northwestern China, according to Next Spaceflight. The payload has not been disclosed.

On Thursday, Nov. 12, ExPace, a subsidiary of the state-owned China Aerospace Science and Industry Corporation (CASIC), is targeting 2:50 p.m. EST for a Kuaizhou 11 launch from Jiuquan, according to Next Spaceflight. Kuaizhou 11 is a solid-fueled rocket designed for fast-turnaround launches of small satellites. Its payload is also undisclosed.

Later Thursday, Nov. 12, SpaceX returns to Vandenberg at 11:23 p.m. EST for USSF-317, another classified Space Force mission on a Falcon 9. The booster is again slated to land on Of Course I Still Love You.

On Friday, Nov. 13, United Launch Alliance (ULA) is targeting 12:54 p.m. EST for Vulcan Leo 1 from Space Launch Complex 41 at Cape Canaveral Space Force Station in Florida. The launch ends a nine-month grounding that followed a nozzle failure during February’s USSF-87 mission; ULA says the boosters now carry a redesigned nozzle. It’s also Vulcan’s first flight for Amazon, carrying 40 satellites for Amazon Leo, the broadband megaconstellation formerly known as Project Kuiper.

Later Friday, Nov. 13, SpaceX opens a 75-minute window at 5:45 p.m. EST for Starship Flight 15 from Pad 2 at Starbase, Texas. It’s the 15th test flight of the company’s reusable megarocket and only its second trip to orbit, after Flight 14’s success in September. About six and a half minutes after liftoff, the Super Heavy booster should return to the launch tower and be caught by its chopstick arms. SpaceX has made that catch before with the rocket’s V1 and V2 boosters, but this will be a first for the new V3 booster. The upper stage, called Ship, will continue to orbit and release 26 of SpaceX’s next-generation Starlink V3 satellites as it did in September.

You can watch the launch live on SpaceX’s website.

The next day, SpaceX will try to catch Ship, too. Until now, Ship’s test flights have ended with a practice landing at sea where the vehicle flips upright, fires its engines, and sets down on the water. This time, after about 14 orbits at roughly 171 miles (275 km) up — a flight of more than 22 hours — Ship must run that same landing maneuver over Starbase, settling into the tower’s arms instead.

SpaceX says Ship will head for the tower only if flight controllers confirm that every system it needs for the catch is prepared. If not, the company can bring Ship down at preplanned spots in the Pacific, with checks built into the flight for splashdowns near Hawaii or off the coast of Chile. If the catch works, Starship will become the first orbital rocket ever recovered in full. On Saturday, Nov. 14, Rocket Lab is targeting 12:30 a.m. EST for The Mountain God Rises, the 100th launch of its Electron rocket, from Launch Complex 1 on New Zealand’s Māhia Peninsula. Electron will deploy a QPS-SAR satellite for the Japanese company iQPS in Rocket Lab’s 11th dedicated launch for the operator. The iQPS constellation uses synthetic aperture radar (SAR), bouncing microwaves off the ground to map Earth’s surface even through rain, smoke, and total darkness.

On Monday, Nov. 2, Rocket Lab launched The Sea God Surveys from New Zealand at 11:41 a.m. EST, delivering another QPS-SAR radar satellite for iQPS. It was Electron’s 99th flight and Rocket Lab’s 10th mission for the Japanese operator. That evening at 7:15 p.m. EST, CAS Space’s Kinetica 2 lifted off from Jiuquan and placed 18 satellites for China’s SpaceSail broadband constellation in polar orbit. It was only the rocket’s second flight, following its debut in March.

On Thursday, Nov. 5, a Long March 2C launched from the Xichang Satellite Launch Center in southwestern China with three Yaogan 51 satellites. Yaogan is the label China gives its government Earth-observation spacecraft, which are widely believed to perform military reconnaissance.

Friday, Nov. 6, brought two launches about an hour apart. Galactic Energy’s Ceres 2 put four satellites in orbit from Jiuquan at 6:37 p.m. EST. Then, after several days of delays, SpaceX launched the classified USSF-432 mission from Vandenberg at 7:48 p.m. EST, landing the booster on Of Course I Still Love You.

On Saturday, Nov. 7, Russia’s Aerospace Forces launched a Soyuz-2.1b from the Plesetsk Cosmodrome in northern Russia. The payload is believed to be a GLONASS satellite for Russia’s version of GPS. The week closed Sunday, Nov. 8, when a Long March 12 lifted off from the Wenchang commercial launch site on Hainan Island at 3:24 p.m. EST, carrying nine more satellites for Guowang, China’s state-run internet megaconstellation.

On Friday, Nov. 20, CASC is targeting 6:00 a.m. EST for a Long March 8A launch from Wenchang, likely carrying another batch of Guowang satellites, according to Next Spaceflight.

On Tuesday, Nov. 24, at 2:00 a.m. EST, a Long March 12 is slated to fly another Guowang group from Wenchang, according to Next Spaceflight.

Brooks Mendenhall is a staff writer at Astronomy, based in Chattanooga, Tennessee, fueled by an unending curiosity about the universe. A former classroom teacher, he has a knack for breaking down complex concepts for a wide audience.
