Our increasingly cluttered skies

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

In the 2013 movie Gravity, Russia destroys its own spy satellite with a missile, creating a cloud of shrapnel and subsequent orbital collisions that rip apart spaceships and astronauts alike. Earth orbit is a place where life imitates art: Eight years later, Moscow launched an actual missile strike on a defunct Soviet satellite, blasting thousands of pieces of metal through space and threatening the International Space Station (ISS) at the nearby altitude of 250 miles (400 kilometers). As the lethal grapeshot sped through space 10 times faster than a rifle bullet, astronauts and cosmonauts huddled in escape pods. Luckily, the debris overshot them, avoiding a real-life Gravity sequel.

This close call was not a lone event. In 2009, history’s first collision between two intact satellites — an American Iridium 33 and a defunct Russian Cosmos-2251 — generated at least 2,000 pieces of debris, some of which passed on uncertain trajectories about 7 miles (11.3 km) from the ISS in March 2012 as crew sheltered in two docked Soyuz craft. And just last year, Chinese crew aboard the Tiangong space station delayed their return to Earth and left their replacements temporarily without an evacuation vehicle when a piece of space junk smaller than 0.04 inch (1 millimeter) hit their docked return capsule. Fallout from these incidents remains in orbit today, along with myriad debris from other adventures. These fragments are collectively known as space junk.

Scientists warn that space junk may be fast approaching a critical threshold that sparks cascading satellite collisions, creating an impassable belt of debris and rendering that region of Earth orbit too dangerous to use. This is called Kessler syndrome, a concept outlined in a 1978 paper by NASA researcher Donald Kessler. Surpassing that threshold is not inevitable, but avoiding it will require considerable engineering and stringent international cooperation.

The most crowded area of space is occupied by the lowest stable Earth orbits, where the equivalent mass of 150 space shuttles currently spins around our planet. Called low Earth orbit (LEO), it is loosely defined as altitudes between 120 miles and 1,200 miles (200 to 2,000 km). LEO is so frequently used because it is the Goldilocks zone where a complete orbit takes two hours or less — high enough to minimize atmospheric drag and stay aloft, yet low enough in the magnetosphere to help shield humans and electronics from radiation damage.

Large constellations of satellites are now being put into LEO. The most numerous of these is SpaceX’s Starlink, whose 10,000+ satellites comprise 66% of LEO traffic. Each rocket carries several dozen satellites weighing as much as a ton each and spanning up to 23 feet (7 meters), providing internet support for remote locations. When they fail, most will reenter the atmosphere and burn up, either within a few weeks of launch if they fail in the initial low orbit, or if they successfully push to their intended higher orbit, within a few years.

But the rate at which LEO is filling up — Starlink had 122 launches in 2025 alone — has some experts worried that we are on the cusp of eventual calamity. “We might not see a substantial change during our own generation, but we will see a steady increase in risks and collisions,” says Hugh Lewis, a University of Birmingham astronautics professor.

Ever since SpaceX launched its first batch of Starlink satellites in 2019, the company has touted its satellites’ avoidance capabilities: Ion-propelled thrusters and autonomous AI guidance systems trigger a Starlink satellite to maneuver away from an object ahead of a close pass if the risk of a collision exceeds a certain threshold. While the industry standard is to maneuver if there is a 1-in-10,000 chance of a collision, SpaceX has tightened its trigger over the years. Today, Starlink satellites take avoiding action if the likelihood of colliding is just 1 in 3.3 million. Based on the current rate of avoidance maneuvers as reported by the company in July 2026, the constellation’s satellites overall are performing more than 400,000 avoidance maneuvers per year.

With other companies and countries joining the LEO space boom — and a lack of coordination between them — SpaceX has taken further action to safeguard its fleet. The company announced in January that it was shifting the bulk of its satellites 13% lower than their original 340-mile (550 km) altitude in large part to reduce aggregate risk, as there are fewer objects that low to collide with. Additionally, lower initial orbits mean failed satellites will naturally deorbit more quickly, preventing them from becoming part of the problem.

OneWeb, a European broadband internet service, sends its satellites to a higher altitude of 750 miles (1,200 km). Because atmospheric drag decreases exponentially with altitude, higher satellites shed drag and gain longevity. But this can generate longer-lasting space junk in the event of failure: At the altitude OneWeb operates, an object could stay in orbit for centuries.

Both OneWeb and Starlink use their own propulsion to deorbit for post-mission disposal, with satellites lasting roughly five years. But if those systems fail, the satellites become space junk, with their orbital altitude determining their longevity.

While operators work to keep space debris in check, external factors can complicate their efforts, either causing orbiting objects to come down prematurely or else increasing the time they’re likely to stay in orbit, crowding the field.

Climate change can cause the latter. When solar photons hit carbon dioxide and other greenhouse gases, the molecules release energy as infrared heat. The troposphere, the atmospheric layer closest to the ground, is sufficiently dense to trap that heat, causing global warming. But higher up, the atmosphere is so thin that radiation escapes, cooling and thinning the mesosphere, our atmosphere’s middle layer. Excessive greenhouse gases can accelerate this process, causing successive atmospheric layers to pancake downward, reducing drag at any given altitude.

According to a study by Lewis’ team, this means satellites that were expected to come down in a couple of years could stay up much longer, quintupling the lifetime of some space debris by the end of this century.

Solar activity has the opposite effect. “In the critical range of 300 to 500 kilometers [190 to 310 miles], the density of the atmosphere is very dependent on whether we’re at solar maximum or solar minimum,” says Jonathan McDowell, an astrophysicist formerly at the Harvard-Smithsonian Center for Astrophysics and now based in the U.K. His decades-long dedication to developing software that catalogs and tracks all rocket launches and reentries has gained him a reputation as the unofficial guru of space traffic and space junk. Each 11-year solar maximum brings an increase in X-ray flux, which ionizes the atmosphere and causes heating, so the atmosphere expands. This, in turn, pushes denser gases higher, increasing drag at the height of LEO. Both effects cause orbiting objects to reenter sooner.

For higher LEO satellites, McDowell says, the question isn’t how many years they are designed to stay in orbit, but how many solar maxima they can survive. For example, the Hubble Space Telescope suffers a pronounced loss of altitude each solar maximum, each bigger than the last as the observatory drops lower and the atmospheric density is greater. Without intervention, it could come down in the next solar maximum.

In a paper accepted for publication in Acta Astronautica, a team led by Samantha Lawler, an astronomer at the University of Regina in Saskatchewan, created a “CRASH clock” characterizing the time it would take for a collision to occur in the absence of all avoidance maneuvers — say, during a “widespread disruptive event” such as a solar storm or massive software failure. (CRASH is an acronym for Collision Realization And Significant Harm.) Once an initial collision takes place, it could cause further, ongoing damage as its debris goes on to spark yet more strikes. Initial calculations based on 2018 satellite traffic allowed 164 days before collisions under the worst circumstances, but as Starlink and other megaconstellations have proliferated, recent calculations bring the countdown to a shocking three days.

While most of the public’s attention is on lower LEO orbits occupied by Starlink, higher LEO altitudes may soon become more crowded as well. SpaceX recently submitted an application to the Federal Communications Commission for space-based data centers with 1 million satellites orbiting much higher than its Starlink fleet. The heavier electrical load of a data center will require vast solar panels and cooling fins, considerably larger than on Starlink satellites. These would also be much more reflective than Starlink, which is already posing serious headaches for astronomers by interfering with observations. But data centers at these altitudes could also create space-junk minefields.

To sustain its million-satellite data center, SpaceX would have to launch about 200,000 satellites each year. If 1% of those fail — the same rate that SpaceX reports for Starlink satellites — “you’re replacing 2,000 satellites a year,” says Lewis. “Those failed satellites alone would meet the critical Kessler threshold, without even looking at the million satellites” making up the functional constellation itself.

Even higher orbits are not immune. Above LEO lies medium Earth orbit (MEO), from 1,240 to 22,000 miles (2,000 to 35,400 km), home to many GPS and navigation satellites. Beyond that sits high Earth orbit (HEO), including geostationary orbit (GEO) directly above Earth’s equator, where satellites sit with 24-hour periods that match Earth’s own rotation. In January, a Russian spy satellite unexpectedly fragmented in an HEO graveyard orbit. The event could have been caused by an impact, suggesting that space junk is infecting even the highest altitudes as well.

The route to addressing orbital crowding requires determining where solutions are most needed. And LEO remains the area most urgently in need of emergency triage. Astronautical engineer Darren McKnight, senior technical officer for LeoLabs, a company that helps coordinate a global network of about a dozen ground-based radars tracking space objects, worries most about LEO orbits above where Starlink — with its state-of-the-art crash-avoidance technology — operates. “I would say that right now we have a contraction of the arteries at about 975 kilometers [600 miles], and if we have a heart attack, it’s going to happen there,” he says.

In the 1970s and ’80s, countries launching spacecraft left rocket bodies and payloads in higher LEO. These large objects have no crash-avoidance capabilities. But this concentration of threats also creates opportunities for reducing risk. “If you take out the 10 worst offenders at 840 and 975 kilometers [520 and 600 miles], you can reduce debris-generating potential by 30%,” McKnight says. These altitudes were two distinct orbital highways historically favored for different missions. Of those top offenders, three are defunct satellites (one European and two Russian) and the rest are rocket stages (one Japanese, two Chinese, one Russian, and three Soviet). That space junk lingers for more than a quarter-century and has the potential to generate even more debris when it is hit, compounding the problem over time. That’s why, McKnight says, removing those worst offenders also offers “much better return on investment” when it comes to resources spent on space junk mitigation — that is, reducing or even retrieving space junk.

Such efforts are gaining traction, but still have a long way to go. Some are managed by national space agencies solely for the purposes of cleaning up their trash, while others are undertaken by private companies purely for profit.

Yet the only true mitigation of space junk to date was in 2022, when the Chinese Shijian-21 satellite retrieved and towed a defunct Beidou-2 G2 navigation satellite from crowded geostationary orbit and parked it in a graveyard orbit 1,865 miles (3,000 km) higher to reduce the risk of collisions. More recently, in 2025, the Shijian-25 satellite launched and docked with fuel-depleted Shijian-21, likely to test refueling capability to prolong its operational lifetime and prevent it from becoming space junk itself. U.S. Space Force officials labeled the maneuver a “priority watch” event due to its potential military use.

Japan-based Astroscale has flown two test missions in LEO that have rendezvoused with targets; its next mission aims to retrieve a OneWeb communications satellite slated for retirement. Other players include Switzerland-based ClearSpace, which is partnering with the European Space Agency, and TransAstra, an aspiring asteroid-mining company based in California. TransAstra has conducted preliminary tests aboard the ISS with a novel capture bag and hopes to launch in 2028 to snag a 100-ton asteroid. That same technology could be adapted for space junk retrieval, especially smaller debris.

For McDowell, the astronomer and spaceflight historian, the satellite boom brings mixed feelings. He wonders if satellite-cluttered skies mean all serious astronomical observing will one day only be done from space telescopes, rather than ground-based observatories. “I am a fan of space exploration,” he says, “but I want us to do it sustainably, in a way that doesn’t trash the environment.”

There is a long way to go. But with the space industry soaring to a value of more than $600 billion, some see the clutter in its wake as an opportunity to profit from clearing space debris out of harm’s way.

Though the sky may be falling, the same genius that placed it there has the capacity to fix it.

Randall Hyman is a science writer whose work has appeared in numerous publications, including Nature, Science, and Smithsonian.

Two days or two weeks – Michael Carrick future decision rolls into the international break

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Manchester United will use the November international break to decide whether Michael Carrick stays on as head coach, after a three-game reprieve that delivered only one win.

According to The Guardian, Carrick came close to being sacked after the 3-0 home defeat to Bournemouth on 25 October.

Director of football Jason Wilcox and chief executive Omar Berrada discussed his position at the training ground the following day and agreed to give him three matches to turn things around before the break.

Those games brought a 2-0 defeat at Chelsea, a 3-2 win over Roma and a chaotic 4-4 draw with Aston Villa at Old Trafford on Sunday. United conceded eight goals across the three fixtures, and defensive frailty remains the biggest worry.

Wilcox is now leading a review of Carrick’s position. He will consult Berrada before making a recommendation to Sir Jim Ratcliffe, who oversees the club’s sporting operations. Talks are expected to begin on Monday and continue through the break.

The league table makes grim reading. United sit in the Premier League relegation zone with just one league win in their last 10 matches.

If he does win a stay of execution, results will need to follow. Manchester United cannot be languishing in 17th place.

Carrick remains defiant. Speaking to Sky Sports News after the Villa draw, he admitted his side “haven’t won enough games” but insisted he expects to move forward.

“I’m quite clear with what direction the club needs to go in, and it’s got to win games, said Carrick after the late Villa draw.

“I think there’s more than enough [to turn things around].”

If United do make a change, former Newcastle manager Eddie Howe is reported to be Wilcox’s preferred candidate. Howe has been out of work since leaving St James’ Park during pre-season, after four and a half years in charge.

In the meantime, there are no plans to strengthen Carrick’s coaching staff.

For now, Carrick is expected to be in charge for United’s next match, against Liverpool. Whether three games were enough to convince the hierarchy should become clear before then.

The trip to Anfield could be do or die for the decorated former United midfielder.

He’s not likely to survive a heavy defeat at the home of the club’s fierce rivals, but a galvanising win would surely buy him time and goodwill.

That’s if he makes it through the next fortnight before then. A decision or indication could be two days or two weeks away.

The only certainty at Old Trafford is uncertainty.

What to Expect at Boulder’s First Sundance Film Festival Coming January 2027

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Sundance Film Festival is coming to Boulder, Colorado, in 2027. (Photo: Hyoung Chang/The Denver Post)

Since 1985, the Sundance Film Festival has been showcasing independent films and drawing thousands of cinema-loving travelers to Park City, Utah. But starting in 2027, Sundance has a new home—the largest independent film festival in the country will be landing in Boulder, Colorado, from January 21 to 31.

Boulderites should brace themselves for a massive influx of visitors; last year, Park City saw 85,472 in-person festival attendees. “The Sundance Film Festival outgrew Park City, maybe 10 or 15 years ago,” Boulder film commissioner Bruce Borowski says. “At some point they had to move just to accommodate the crowds and the success of the festival.”

As a local, I’ve been able to witness the mounting anticipation firsthand. I was present at the celebratory press conference at Boulder Theater in March 2025 when Governor Jared Polis stated, “What excites me the most is that we are opening the door in Colorado for artists from across the world to showcase their stories and to become part of our story.” It’s a big win for the city both culturally and economically, but it’s also an opportunity for visitors to experience the all-around appeal of Boulder. Between attending film screenings and rubbing elbows with the filmmakers and actors in town, there are award-winning restaurants to enjoy, mountainous terrain to hike and ski, and can’t-miss tourist hot spots to explore.

The decision to bring Sundance to Boulder was based largely on the city’s natural beauty and creative community, which align with the original vision of the festival. Aside from being home to Sundance founder Robert Redford’s alma mater, CU Boulder, the city’s walkable downtown and convenient location make it a great fit for the event.

“Being located near an international airport, Boulder is really easy to get to,” explains Cris Jones, director of strategic partnerships for the city of Boulder “It’s right up against the mountains, and you get to have a mountain experience—without having to go over any mountain passes to get here.”

Unlike the festival experience in Park City, where film screenings and activations were more spread out, the Boulder experience will be more centralized; all venues—which include Boulder Theater, Chautauqua Auditorium, and eTown Hall—will be within about a two-mile radius.

“It’s more connected and accessible,” Paula DuPré Pesmen, managing director of festival and institute operations at Sundance Institute says. “It will be easy to get around from venue to venue, from panels to films, from restaurants to shops.” People can take advantage of dedicated Sundance Film Festival shuttles, along with free HOP bus service, shared e-bike passes, and free Regional Transportation District (RTD) transportation to and from Boulder. Once you’re downtown, most venues and activities are within walking distance of each other, and bus routes will encompass  the hotels in Boulder to pick up and drop off festivalgoers.

Along with the perks of having Sundance in Boulder, there are challenges expected as well, such as increased traffic and larger crowds at restaurants and businesses. And while Boulder has proven its capacity to host large events, the issue of lodging has come up. “The Boulder Creek Fest brings in over 90,000 people in one day, and the audience expected for Sundance is around 90,000 people over 11 days,”  Charlene Hoffman, CEO of Visit Boulder, says. “The challenge that we’ve had, and that we’ve worked through for the most part, is the lodging component, because housing that many people over 11 days is new for us.”

Hoffman shares that the city has helped residents in the community get their short-term rental licenses in order to rent out their homes. These listings can be found on Airbnb, Vrbo, and other home rental sites. Travelers are encouraged to look into lodging beyond Boulder County as well, such as Denver and Estes Park, both of which will be running free shuttles to Boulder.

Whether you plan to attend screenings or not, there’s plenty to eat, drink, and do in Boulder as the inaugural festival kicks off.

In between film screenings, recaffeinate at one of the many cafes in town, like Boulder-founded Ozo Coffee Roasters (located on both the east and west sides of Pearl Street). Or pop by my favorite coffee shop with a view, Spruce Confections on Pearl’s west end, and mingle with festivalgoers over fresh-baked pastries. “That’s always been the magic for me when going to the festival,” Pesmen says. “You find yourself in conversations with people in lines at coffee shops and restaurants, and you talk about things that you heard or a film that you saw.”

With over 400 restaurants in Boulder, options range from gourmet dining to fast-casual, depending on how you’re feeling after a day of watching films. For upscale Northern Italian fare, make reservations at the Michelin-starred, James Beard Award-winning Frasca. Or grab a bite at The Sink, the more than 100-year-old institution on College Hill. Not only does it have the best burger in Boulder (I can vouch for this), but it was once the workplace of Sundance founder Robert Redford. In 1955, he was a CU Boulder student working as the restaurant’s janitor.

Finally, cap off the night with cocktails at License No. 1, a speakeasy-themed lounge beneath the historic Hotel Boulderado. For creative tiki drinks and tropical decor, duck into Jungle and feel winter’s chill melt away.

Boulder’s natural scenery—particularly the iconic Flatirons formations jutting from the foothills—is even more breathtaking when dusted in glittering snow. Play in the snow at Nederland’s Eldora Mountain Resort, just 21 miles from downtown Boulder. Catch the hourly Ski-n-Ride shuttle (a day pass costs $5.50) from Downtown Boulder Station to ski the slopes or snowshoe the Nordic Center’s groomed trails. Discuss film screenings on a crisp, invigorating hike through pine-scented air at Chautauqua Park (I’m partial to the Bluebell Mesa Trail). For a mellow walk with rewarding views of white-frosted peaks, meander along the Bobolink Trail on the south side of town.

When you’re ready to head indoors, warm up with a hot beverage at the Dushanbe Teahouse and take in the beautifully intricate interior, which was crafted by artisans in Tajikistan, then shipped and assembled here in Boulder. Another tea attraction not to be missed: a tour of the Celestial Seasonings factory. Busy festival attendees in need of some relaxation can book a massage or facial at the St. Julien Spa. And of course, your time in Boulder isn’t complete without strolling the four-block outdoor pedestrian mall on Pearl Street, which will still be glowing with holiday lights. “Just being around during Sundance, even without a ticket, is a really great experience,” Hoffman says. “There’s so much energy.”

Paige Triola has been a Boulder enthusiast since moving to the city in 2019. She wrote an exhaustive guide on Boulder for U.S. News & World Report, and is eager to see the Sundance Film Festival thrive in its new setting.

Selfless Deed Paradox: Why Doing Good Matters More Than Pure Motives

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A positive psychology and linguistic investigation from The Ohio State University challenges the popular assumption that good deeds lose moral value if the giver gains personal satisfaction or social praise. Testing perceptions across seven situational dimensions, researchers discovered that everyday observers view the terms “nice” and “kind” as largely interchangeable, while reserving “caring” for emotionally warm, planned, and relational actions.

Crucially, the findings show that people rarely discount helpful acts due to mixed motives, supporting evolutionary perspectives that the warm glow of altruism evolved as Mother Nature’s primary reinforcement mechanism to promote prosocial cooperation.

Source: The Ohio State University / International Journal of Applied Positive Psychology

In a well-known 1998 episode of the sitcom Friends, the character Phoebe Buffay sets out to prove to Joey Tribbiani that truly selfless good deeds exist. Her efforts unravel when an anonymous donation to a cause she dislikes yields an unexpected burst of personal happiness, forcing her to concede defeat: “He’s right. There are no selfless good deeds.”

This philosophical debate has increasingly permeated modern cultural discussions, particularly among younger generations who argue that a sharp moral divide separates being “nice” from being “kind.” Under this view, “niceness” is dismissed as superficial politeness or reputation management, whereas “kindness” demands pure, selfless intent.

However, a study published in the International Journal of Applied Positive Psychology by psychologists and linguists at The Ohio State University reveals that this cynical skepticism does not reflect everyday human perception.

Led by doctoral researcher Ally Heiland, Professor of Clinical Psychology Jennifer Cheavens, and Professor of Developmental Psychology and Linguistics Laura Wagner, the study demonstrates that ordinary people do not discount helpful acts simply because the helper gains joy or social standing in return.

The investigation grew out of discussions in Cheavens’ positive psychology seminars, where students argued that performing good deeds out of social obligation or to bolster one’s reputation would be viewed negatively by others.

To systematically test how linguistic framing and situational context shape moral evaluation, the researchers drafted 14 experimental vignettes. The scenarios modeled seven core dimensions of prosocial action:

Participants were asked to place themselves on the receiving end of everyday occurrences, such as having a door held open or having a meal paid for by another customer, and rate each act on how “kind,” “nice,” and “caring” it felt.

The empirical data directly contradicted the idea that people view “nice” and “kind” as fundamentally opposed. Across six of the seven situational dimensions, participants evaluated acts as equally kind and nice. The only divergence occurred regarding cost: while high- and low-cost behaviors were rated as equally kind, participants endorsed high-cost sacrifices as significantly “nicer.”

“Participants rated scenarios as similarly kind and nice across six of the seven situational dimensions we tested,” the authors observed.

In contrast to the interchangeability of kind and nice, the word “caring” occupied a distinct psychological tier.

Helpful actions performed between individuals who shared an existing personal bond were rated as markedly more “caring,” yet no kinder or nicer than the identical acts performed by unfamiliar strangers. Furthermore, actions that demanded greater personal sacrifice (high cost) were perceived as both nicer and more caring, but not necessarily kinder.

“It’s a word that when we’re using it often indicates some sort of closeness or intention that might not be naturally baked into something like kind or nice,” explained lead author Ally Heiland.

Cheavens noted that the distinction stems from the grammatical and affective roots of the word: “People don’t say, ‘I feel kind.’ But you say, ‘I care for you.’ Caring sets a higher bar because it’s the closest of the three terms to an emotion.”

Across all dimensions, acts were judged most favorably when they met three conditions: they were delivered with genuine interpersonal warmth, planned in advance, and went beyond what was already socially expected of the giver.

The overarching finding of the study provides reassurance against social cynicism: observers overwhelmingly perceive prosocial behavior as positive, rarely second-guessing the internal motivations of the giver.

“The biggest effect was like, yep, people think that’s good when you do good things,” said Cheavens.

Rather than viewing the emotional satisfaction of helping as proof of disguised selfishness, cognitive scientists argue that this “warm glow” serves an evolutionary purpose.

“The idea that you do get a warm, fuzzy feeling might actually be part of how Mother Nature motivates us to be nice to other people,” explained co-author Laura Wagner. Without immediate neurobiological reward systems reinforcing cooperative acts, maintaining large-scale prosocial behavior across human societies would be evolutionarily unsustainable.

The researchers conclude that reducing anxiety over whether an act is perceived as “perfectly selfless” could help encourage more daily community action, eliminating linguistic barriers and self-consciousness that prevent people from reaching out to help others.

Do people judge good deeds negatively if the giver benefits from them?

No. The Ohio State University study found that everyday observers and recipients do not devalue good deeds simply because the giver gains personal happiness, social praise, or a boost in reputation. Prosocial behavior was consistently rated as positive across contexts.

What is the difference between “nice,” “kind,” and “caring”?

The study found that “nice” and “kind” are treated as practically interchangeable across most everyday situations. However, “caring” sets a much higher psychological bar: it is reserved for acts that involve pre-existing relationships, emotional warmth, planning, and higher personal cost.

Why does helping others make us feel good?

Cognitive and evolutionary psychology suggest that the “warm glow” of doing good is an adaptive biological reinforcement mechanism. Experiencing positive emotions after helping others evolved to motivate humans to maintain the cooperative behaviors essential for group survival.

Funding: The study was funded by the Center for HumanKindness at the Columbus Foundation.

Kind, Nice, and Caring, Oh My! Investigating Differences in How Individuals Interpret Prosocial Language

Although prosocial behaviors benefit both actors and recipients, individuals report engaging in these behaviors less frequently than they would like. One potential barrier is that individuals may perceive common prosocial terms, such as kind, nice, and caring, differently. Although these terms are often used interchangeably, differing interpretations may influence willingness to engage in prosocial behaviors.

This study addresses two questions: (1) are there differences in how individuals perceive the terms kind, nice, and caring, and (2) what contextual characteristics lead individuals to characterize behaviors as kind, nice, or caring versus not kind, nice, nor caring? Participants (N = 736) were presented with seven vignettes depicting prosocial behavior that varied on contextual dimensions associated with perceptions of prosociality (e.g., warmth, cost, anonymity).

Participants rated each vignette on whether the person performing the behavior was kind, nice, and caring. Multilevel modeling analyses revealed no significant differences in perceptions of kind and nice, while individuals had a higher threshold for labeling behaviors as caring. Regression analyses revealed that across all three terms, behaviors characterized as higher in warmth and planning and lower in expected obligation were rated as more prosocial. Higher cost behaviors were perceived as nicer and more caring, while behaviors directed toward known recipients were rated as more caring.

These findings suggest that although kind, nice, and caring are perceived similarly, there are varying situational differences that may assist with promoting prosocial behavior through using terms that align with the expectations of prosocial programs and interventions.

These 8 Common Heating Mistakes Could Be Costing You More Than You Realize, Experts Warn

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Staying cozy is a top priority in winter—whether you're curling up on the sofa or getting snug under the covers. But turning up the heating isn’t always the answer: some everyday habits can leave your home colder while pushing your bills higher.

From setting the thermostat too high to neglecting to bleed radiators, small heating mistakes can affect how warm your home feels and how much energy you use.

To help you stay comfortable without wasting energy, we asked heating experts to share the most common mistakes—and the simple fixes that can make your home warmer and your bills easier to manage.

When the chill sets in, it’s tempting to whack the thermostat up a few notches for a quick blast of warmth. But this common habit does more harm than good. “While it won’t heat your home any faster, what it will do is waste energy and therefore waste money,” say the heating specialist experts at Heatforce. “Each degree above 66 degrees Fahrenheit can add a whopping 10 percent to your annual heating costs.”

The smarter approach is to keep your thermostat at around 66 degrees when you’re at home and awake, then drop it by four to six degrees overnight. That way, you’ll stay comfortable without sending your bills soaring.

When arranging furniture, it’s easy to prioritize style over function—but placing bulky pieces too close to your radiator can drastically reduce its efficiency.

“Make sure that furniture and curtains aren’t obstructing this area of your home, as heat needs to be able to circulate freely,” says Amthal Karim, head of design at Furniture & Choice. “Where space allows, move sofas, armchairs, and other large pieces of furniture away from radiators to help warm air circulate more effectively around the room.”

Scott Byrom, a home energy expert at The Energy Shop, adds: “A large piece of furniture placed against a radiator can absorb a significant amount of the heat it emits, meaning you're paying to warm the back of your sofa rather than the room. Pulling furniture just six inches away allows warm air to rise and move freely, and you'll feel the difference almost immediately.”

Warm air can escape through gaps around doors, windows, and letterboxes, leaving rooms colder and forcing your heating to work harder—which can push up your bills over winter.

“Many older homes with hardwood front doors will experience worse drafts in the winter as they are susceptible to expanding in heat and contracting in the cold, meaning gaps around the frame will become even wider,” warns Oliver Novakovic, head of innovation at David Wilson Homes. “To combat this, invest in a draft exclusion runner to go along the bottom of your door. You can also purchase lengths of gap sealer to run around the frame, preventing cold air from blowing in around the door frame.”

Draft blockers, letterbox brushes, and simple sealant strips are all inexpensive fixes that can help a home retain heat far more effectively.

If you’re relying on an old heated blanket to stay warm, check its age and condition. It may no longer heat as effectively, leaving you tempted to turn up the thermostat and spend more on heating.

“Older electric blankets or those without overheating protection can pose a fire hazard,” says Adele Brennan, home electrical buyer at Vonhaus. “However, there are newer, innovative electric blankets that come equipped with an automatic shut-off function.”

A newer blanket with an automatic shut-off function is a safer choice, but using one to compensate for a cold room won’t fix the underlying heating problem.

It might feel indulgent to keep the heating running from dawn until dusk, but it’s an expensive habit that can quickly inflate your energy bills. “Instead, schedule your heating for convenient times,” says Stephen Hankinson, heating expert at Electric Radiators Direct.

“For example, if you like being comfortable as you get ready in the morning, program your heating to switch on just before you wake up and then off again by the time you leave.”

This way, you’ll enjoy warmth exactly when you need it, without wasting energy. Just don’t forget to adjust your schedule when the clocks change.

It's easy to focus on simply switching radiators on and off, but regular maintenance is just as important. Bleeding your radiators is essential for keeping them running efficiently. “After a while, pockets of air can get trapped within the radiators, causing cold spots and preventing hot water from flowing through,” explains Hankinson. “The usual sign your radiator needs bleeding is that the top section is much cooler than the bottom.”

To fix this, switch off your heating system, use a radiator key to open the valve, and keep a towel handy to catch any drips of water. Doing this at least once a year will keep your heating even and effective.

Drying clothes on radiators is common in colder, wetter months, but it can stop heat from circulating around the room. Your heating may then need to stay on longer to reach a comfortable temperature, using more energy and pushing up your bills. A clothes dryer in a well-ventilated room is a better option.

“The bigger issues are its contribution to mold and dampness within your home, as well as the inability to heat your home, so you're left with a damp, cold house throughout winter, which is costing you,” says Andy Simms from MyBuilder.com. “Instead, using a clothes drying rail or taking advantage of brighter, milder days to hang your washing outside or open windows can make a real difference. And when drying clothes indoors, using a dehumidifier is effective in keeping moisture low.”

It might feel inconvenient to book in a boiler service—especially if it means rearranging your day to be at home—but skipping it can be a costly mistake, as minor issues could go unnoticed, which could reduce its efficiency.

For peace of mind, it’s best to get your service done in autumn. That way, any small problems are caught early, and you’ll avoid the risk of being left without reliable heating during the coldest months of the year.

“Having your boiler serviced annually is a great action to take to prevent boiler breakdowns, as well as improve its efficiency. It helps keep your boiler operating at maximum efficiency, consuming less fuel to generate the same amount of heat,” says Ian Palmer-Smith, appliance repair expert at Domestic & General.

Is Your Cycling Computer Lying to You?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Your power meter might be working perfectly, but that doesn’t mean your cycling computer records data accurately. Here’s why you should care and how you can check.

Spend enough time comparing power meters, and you’ll quickly learn that they don’t always agree. Put a power meter on your crank and another on your pedals, and the numbers may not match exactly. That can be frustrating when you’re trying to figure out which one is right, but for most riders, a small, consistent difference isn’t necessarily a problem.

If you use a power meter for training, what matters most is that the numbers you’re seeing today are comparable to the ones you saw last week or last month. Whether your threshold is actually 250 watts or 255 watts matters less than knowing that an interval at that power represents the same effort from one ride to the next.

But what if your power meter isn’t the only thing affecting those numbers? What if the bike computer recording your ride is changing the data before you ever upload it to Strava or TrainingPeaks?

I recently noticed something odd while testing a cycling computer. At first, I was doing this to compare GPS track accuracy, but since I was testing, I also paired all the devices to my heart rate strap and Quarq power meter. When I compared the ride files afterward, what stood out was that their power numbers didn’t match. Initially, the differences were small enough. But when I looked more closely, I noticed it immediately. Every time I stopped pedaling, one computer still recorded power.

Before I get into what the heck sticky watts are, I should say I first learned about the issue in 2025 when I saw a video from Shane Miller, who goes by GPLama on YouTube. The video is called “Power Meter Data Recording: The Wild West?”, and it’s worth watching. Shane was the first person I saw digging into this particular recording issue with cycling computers.

So what the heck is it? Sticky watts happen when a cycling computer keeps recording a power value for a few seconds after you’ve stopped pedaling. Instead of dropping to zero, the last power number gets “stuck” in your ride file until the computer finally catches up.

Let’s say you’re riding along at 250 watts and stop pedaling as you approach a corner. Your computer might continue recording 250 watts for a few seconds while you’re coasting. On a graph, that can look like a flat line extending beyond the point where the power should have dropped.

A few seconds might not sound like much, but think about how often you stop pedaling on a typical ride. Every corner, descent, stop sign, or moment spent coasting in the group is another opportunity for those extra watts to show up in your file. And if you stop pedaling after a hard effort, the computer could be holding a much higher number than 250 watts, which can skew your data.

It’s also worth distinguishing this from the setting on some computers that lets you exclude zeroes when calculating average power. That setting changes how an average is calculated. Sticky watts affect the power values recorded in the ride file itself, so those extra values can still be there when you upload the ride for analysis.

On one ride, I compared the DURA 2 with a Garmin Edge 850 and an Edge 1060. The two Garmins recorded almost identical power numbers, but the DURA 2 consistently came out higher. When I looked more closely at the files, I found repeated instances where the COROS continued recording the last power value after both Garmins had dropped to zero.

The difference was especially noticeable during a series of short, hard efforts. After one effort, the DURA 2 recorded 565 watts for four seconds while both Garmins recorded zero. After another, it held 656 watts for three seconds. Across a roughly three-minute section containing several of these efforts, the Garmins averaged about 260 watts, while the COROS recorded nearly 307 watts.

Those short efforts make the issue easy to see, but the difference showed up across the ride, too. In the roughly 98 minutes of matched power data I compared, the DURA 2 averaged about 186 watts, while both Garmins averaged about 179 watts.

On a second ride, I compared the DURA 2 with a Hammerhead Karoo, Wahoo ELEMNT ACE, and Garmin Edge 850. Again, the COROS recorded higher power through a section with several hard efforts followed by coasting. The Garmin recorded lower numbers than the Karoo and Wahoo in that section, which reminds us that differences can go both ways when computers record the same power meter.

I was honestly surprised to see this on the DURA 2. The original DURA had similar issues early on, but its power recording improved with subsequent updates, and it performed well in GPLama’s 2025 video presenting the issue. I don’t want to turn this into a story about picking on COROS. The company has a good history of updating and improving its products, and I expect it can address this issue on the DURA 2 as well. For now, these files show why it’s worth paying attention to what your cycling computer records, even when you trust your power meter.

If you mostly ride for fun and don’t pay much attention to your power data afterward, a few extra watts in your ride file probably won’t change your life. But if you use that data to track your training, compare rides, and work with a coach, those differences can matter.

The big problem is that sticky watts don’t necessarily add the same amount to every ride. A steady climb with little coasting might show no difference. But a ride with lots of corners, stop signs, descents, or short, hard efforts followed by coasting could produce a much larger one. That makes it harder to compare rides or judge how much work you actually did.

The easiest way to check is to record the same ride on two cycling computers paired to the same power meter. You don’t necessarily need to buy a second computer. If a friend has one you can borrow, pair both to your power meter and record a ride with a few clear transitions from pedaling to coasting.

Alternatively, you can look closely at the power files your head unit generates. Do a couple of hard efforts, like sprints, where you stop pedaling at the end, then zoom in on those sections. Look for a flat section where one computer continues recording the same power value for several seconds after the other has dropped to zero.

And yes, I know that I’m definitely in some deep weeds over here, and it can be tough to argue that a cycling computer recording a few extra watts is a huge problem worth tackling. For many riders, it might not matter at all. But the current crop of cycling computers is also the most expensive we’ve ever seen. These devices are marketed and sold as precision training tools, and accurate data recording is central to that. These devices should do that, and riders shouldn’t have to question it.

Test Editor Dan Chabanov got his start in cycling as a New York City bike messenger but quickly found his way into road and cyclocross racing, competing in professional cyclocross races from 2009 to 2019 and winning a Master’s National Championship title in 2018. Prior to joining Bicycling in 2021, Dan worked as part of the race organization for the Red Hook Crit, as a coach with EnduranceWERX, as well as a freelance writer and photographer.

Water Fluoridation Does Not Impair Brain Function

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A population-based epidemiological investigation from Trinity College Dublin finds no evidence connecting lifetime exposure to fluoridated drinking water with cognitive decline or dementia risk in older adults. Published in the Journal of Dental Research, the study analyzed 2,495 adults from the Irish Longitudinal Study on Ageing (TILDA), mapping their residential histories against historic national water fluoride records dating back to 1964. Cognitive test scores across low-, moderate-, and high-exposure cohorts showed no significant differences, providing empirical reassurance regarding the neurological safety of community water fluoridation policies.

Source: Trinity College Dublin / Journal of Dental Research

Community water fluoridation (CWF) has served as a cornerstone of preventive public dental health for more than six decades, drastically reducing the prevalence of dental caries and tooth loss across entire populations. In Ireland, mandatory fluoridation of public water supplies was introduced under the Health (Fluoridation of Water Supplies) Act of 1960 and fully implemented in 1964, establishing one of the most comprehensive nationwide programs in the world.

Despite decades of documented dental benefits, water fluoridation has faced renewed public debate, fueled by concerns that systemic fluoride ingestion might cross the blood-brain barrier and exert neurotoxic effects or contribute to neurodegenerative decline later in life. However, much of the existing literature has relied on ecological comparisons, cross-sectional snapshots, or regions with naturally extreme, non-regulated fluoride concentrations.

To provide clear, longitudinal epidemiological clarity, researchers at Trinity College Dublin evaluated lifetime exposure data from the Irish Longitudinal Study on Ageing (TILDA), tracking whether decades of consuming fluoridated tap water correlates with impaired cognitive function in older age.

Their findings, published in the Journal of Dental Research, show no link between lifelong community water fluoridation and cognitive performance.

Led by Dr. Vinay Sharma and senior investigator Professor Lina Zgaga from Trinity’s School of Medicine, the research team analyzed a representative cohort of 2,495 community-dwelling older adults (mean age 65 years) enrolled in Wave 3 of TILDA.

Rather than assuming uniform exposure, the investigators reconstructed granular personal histories:

Cognitive functioning was systematically measured using two standardized neuropsychological batteries widely deployed in clinical dementia research: the Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE) and the Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA).

The comparative analysis showed that cognitive scores did not differ between exposure groups. Participants with continuous, high-volume exposure to fluoridated municipal water performed on par with peers who had consumed unfluoridated private well water.

“Our findings provide reassurance that lifetime exposure to fluoridated drinking water at the levels used in Ireland is not associated with poorer cognitive function in older adults,” said Dr. Vinay Sharma, lead author of the study. “This is an important public health question, and we hope these findings contribute to an evidence-based conversation about the safety of the community water fluoridation programme in Ireland and internationally.”

Ireland’s longstanding national policy provides an ideal epidemiological setting to assess long-term health outcomes.

“Ireland is unusual because it has a national policy of community water fluoridation. This creates an opportunity to do high-quality research in large studies and answer many outstanding questions around fluoridation,” noted Professor Lina Zgaga, Principal Investigator of the study. “Having good, contemporary evidence is essential to support informed decisions about this public health intervention nationally and internationally, and to ensure that policy continues to be based on the best available evidence.”

Co-Principal Investigator Dr. Michael Crowe emphasized the real-world value of these findings: “Fluoridation has been one of the most effective tools we have for reducing tooth decay at a population level, so it matters that we now have long-term Irish evidence that it isn’t costing anything in terms of cognitive health.”

The researchers note certain structural constraints. Total personal fluoride intake is influenced by dietary sources (such as tea consumption) and fluoridated dentifrice (toothpaste) ingestion, variables not captured in municipal water records. Furthermore, because the national program commenced in 1964, only 5% of this older adult cohort were under five years old when fluoridation began, limiting statistical power to evaluate early childhood exposure.

Nevertheless, public health experts emphasize that continuous safety monitoring remains essential.

“Nonetheless, we should continue to check for any adverse effects of water fluoridation in the Irish population on an ongoing basis. This research establishes a way to monitor the health of water consumers but should just be the baseline for future studies,” stated co-author Professor Brian O’Connell.

By confirming that regulated, therapeutic community water fluoridation does not compromise cognitive health, the study offers key longitudinal evidence supporting evidence-based public dental policies worldwide.

Did lifetime exposure to fluoridated water affect cognitive scores in older adults?

No. Testing nearly 2,500 older Irish adults across multiple exposure tiers showed that individuals with decades of high fluoride exposure performed identically on standardized cognitive tests (MMSE and MoCA) compared to those with low or no lifetime exposure.

How did this study differ from previous research on fluoride and brain health?

Most prior studies examined fluoride exposure across narrow, isolated timeframes (only childhood or short adult periods) or relied on broad regional assumptions. This study tracked full residential histories over several decades and matched them to historical municipal water monitoring archives, establishing individual lifetime exposure estimates.

Did early childhood exposure show any link to late-life cognitive decline?

The researchers found no evidence that childhood exposure harmed cognition later in life. However, because Ireland’s national program began in 1964, only 5% of participants were young children when the initiative launched, meaning larger future cohorts will be needed to confirm early-life outcomes.

Funding: The present analysis was funded by the Health Research Board (Ireland) Secondary Data Analysis Project Award. Data were used from Wave 3 of TILDA (2014–2015), which was funded by the Department of Health (Ireland) and Atlantic Philanthropies. The collection of historical address data and the estimation of lifetime fluoride exposure for TILDA participants was supported by a Health Research Board (Ireland) Applied Partnership Award.

Lifetime Fluoride Exposure and Cognition in Older Irish Adults

Community water fluoridation (CWF) is an effective public health intervention to prevent dental caries. This study investigated potential associations between lifetime fluoride exposure from CWF and cognitive outcomes in older Irish adults and whether early-life exposure modified these associations.

Data from the Irish Longitudinal Study on Ageing (TILDA; wave 3, 2014 to 2015) were analyzed (n = 6,684). Lifetime (≥50 y) and early-life (ages 0 to 5 y) fluoride exposures were estimated by linking residential histories to national water fluoride monitoring data and were available for N = 2,495 participants (mean age 65.32 y, standard deviation 8.64 y).

Primary exposures were cumulative lifetime exposure to fluoridated water (CLEFW; continuous) and percentage lifetime exposure to fluoridated water (%LEFW), classified as low (<25%), medium (25% to 75%), or high (>75%). Cognitive outcomes were Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE) and Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA) error scores (30 minus raw scores), with higher scores indicating poorer cognition. Associations were examined using multivariable negative binomial regression models, adjusted for sociodemographic, lifestyle, health, loneliness, and health care access factors.

Effect modification by early-life fluoride exposure (any/none) was tested using interaction terms. CLEFW showed no association with cognitive errors (incidence rate ratio [IRR]MMSE = 1.00, 95% confidence interval [CI]: 0.99 to 1.01; IRRMoCA = 1.00, 95% CI: 1.00 to 1.01). In unadjusted models, the high-exposure %LEFW group had lower cognition errors than the low-exposure reference group (IRRMMSE = 0.62, 95% CI: 0.49 to 0.79; IRRMoCA = 0.72, 95% CI: 0.62 to 0.83), but these became nonsignificant in fully adjusted models (IRRMMSE = 1.17, 95% CI: 0.88 to 1.56; IRRMoCA = 1.10, 95% CI: 0.94 to 1.29).

There was no evidence of effect modification by early-life fluoride exposure for either cognitive outcome (pinteraction > 0.05). Lifetime fluoride exposure from CWF was not associated with later-life cognitive function in older Irish adults, and there was no statistical evidence of effect modification by early-life exposure.

This Former NASA Engineer Has 85 Million YouTube Subscribers. He Says This Is How to Differentiate Your Videos From AI Slop.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

The YouTube star explains what makes content valuable as it gets cheaper to produce.

He rose to fame with YouTube videos like How to Survive a Grenade Blast, which has 100 million views, and Can you swim in Jello?, which has 224 million views.

In a new interview with Business Insider, Rober said the solution to competing against AI slop, or low-quality AI posts, is to go out into the real world and do something noteworthy.

“Do you want to see a simulation of someone dunking a basketball with a sick trick, or do you want to see someone actually do it?” he said. “The fact that you know it’s real is what makes it crazy.”

Rober believes that quality sets his channel apart.

“At least for a while, I think I have a moat on the YouTube channel,” he said. He believes that his real-world experiments and recognizable human personality give his channel an advantage over AI-generated content — at least, for now.

Rober said his YouTube videos generated about $17 million in revenue last year, which is roughly what it cost to produce them. He also makes money through his company CrunchLabs, which sells products like maker kits that introduce young people to science and engineering.

Rober noted that his business now employs more than 200 people. Its mission centers on inspiring curiosity and learning.

“Curiosity is so important in this new age of AI,” Rober said. “It means you just have a lifetime of learning.”

AI introduces a conundrum for content creators. The tools that make videos easier to create are also making it harder for individual channels to stand out. For some creators, AI means more growth. Others say it could cause their work to get lost in the crowd.

One popular use case of AI is to help creators introduce existing videos to audiences who speak different languages. For example, translation startup Linguana builds and manages local YouTube channels with AI dubbing. It includes translated thumbnails and descriptions, providing creators with a way to expand internationally without doing all the translation work themselves.

Services like Linguana can allow creators to generate additional income from content they have already created. In April 2025, Linguana told Business Insider that it was generating 10,000 video translations each month for hundreds of YouTubers. The company takes half the revenue generated through its partnerships rather than charging upfront.

However, easier production ushers in a competitive challenge. Rober said that when more people create similar content, technical execution alone may become less valuable. This means that channels built around anonymous narration or animation could face particular challenges as competitors produce content faster and more cheaply.

It’s Time to Confront the Realities of What Your CRM Can Do — and What It Can’t

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

CRMs can be powerful tools, but they’re not crystal balls.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

In the world of franchise recruitment, nothing is more sacred than a quality lead. And at any given time, there are potentially millions of Americans considering an entrepreneurial future, some of whom have always harbored a desire to own and operate a business of their own and others for whom circumstances dictated taking matters into their own hands. Both of these would-be entrepreneurial types are invaluable, as you never know which one may go on to sign an official franchise agreement.

That means tracking every lead is paramount. The majority of franchise sales and development teams are highly reliant on their Customer Relationship Management (CRM) systems to achieve this important task. Thanks to today’s technologies, now fully integrated with the power of AI, CRM databases have become much more complex in both design and functionality. Much thought and consideration go into these factors, providing development teams with what they hope will be an efficient roadmap to meet their outsized recruitment goals.

But there’s a catch. Regardless of how much time, capital and manpower sales and development teams pour into their proprietary CRM systems, they’re not exactly infallible. And that’s where the true issue lies. Accepting this reality means you understand the limitations, making now as good a time as any to confront the reality of what your CRM can do.

Whether you’re starting from scratch or looking to upgrade your CRM’s capabilities, there are some essential factors to consider in finding the right fit for your organization. Ideally, you’re looking to establish a critical technological component of your franchise recruitment effort, a centralized database of candidates that will come to define your “quantity vs. quality” approach.

When assessing your specific needs in a CRM, there are several rhetorical questions involved. But regardless of your organization’s size or its recruitment strategy, your CRM should be designed with long-term value in mind. Here is what’s most important to consider if you want to get started on the right foot:

Once you’ve defined these critical components, your sales and development team is ready to progress to the procurement stage. This is not the time to scrimp on your due diligence, so take the necessary time to evaluate your CRM options and pay close attention to the demos offered by different technology providers.

The very title of this article establishes that CRMs are not the panacea for your franchise recruitment efforts. That’s because, despite the advanced automated capabilities baked into the most technologically advanced CRM, there are still things it cannot accomplish for you. These can be broken down into three key areas:

1. Activity tracking does not guarantee pipeline health

By design, the majority of advanced CRM setups are designed to capture data, manage incoming leads and keep track of real-time updates and activity. And they do a fine job with these capabilities — but what they can’t do is provide your sales and development teams with the predictive analytics necessary to make informed decisions on how to proceed. In other words, it can’t inherently show you the health and viability of your current sales pipeline.

2. Critical datasets may be siloed elsewhere

Even with the most advanced capabilities, including those powered by AI, CRMs can’t truly assess the total investment you’ve made in front-end lead generation and tie that back to long-term backend performance. That’s because the valuable insights you need to make informed decisions may not be readily available in the CRM, but scattered across other networks … even external ones.

In a typical knee-jerk reaction, franchise sales and development teams that miss the mark on their intended recruiting goals immediately go into finger-pointing mode. When signings fall short, the CRM often gets the blame. Before replacing it, examine how your team defines the process, maintains the data and follows through with candidates. But the issue here is franchise development teams mistaking their CRM as a structural sales methodology, when in fact success calls for a more disciplined, human-centric approach.

Franchise recruitment teams can be quick to blame CRM systems when their lead-generation goals go unmet. So, they take the most drastic action first and decide to change it out for something shinier, better and — in almost every instance — more expensive. But even after all those changes, and the requisite amount of time goes by, they find themselves in the exact same situation down the road.

That’s because the standards they’re applying to measure results aren’t where the actual problems lie. More often than not, the real problem is in how they’re utilizing the CRM system and what they’re asking it to accomplish.

By design, CRMs are powerful systems that do an admirable job doing exactly what we program them to do — to the letter. They track activity, manage leads, store critical information for later retrieval, and can provide us with real-time updates of where every candidate is in the process. They keep the pipeline moving smoothly and efficiently. But never mistake that for visibility or predictive analytics.

CRMs are not infallible, and they’re definitely not the crystal ball you’re looking for when it comes to predicting the long-term success of your franchise recruitment goals. Before you blame your CRM, take a hard look at your recruitment process, the quality of your data and who is accountable for moving each candidate forward.

In the world of franchise recruitment, nothing is more sacred than a quality lead. And at any given time, there are potentially millions of Americans considering an entrepreneurial future, some of whom have always harbored a desire to own and operate a business of their own and others for whom circumstances dictated taking matters into their own hands. Both of these would-be entrepreneurial types are invaluable, as you never know which one may go on to sign an official franchise agreement.

That means tracking every lead is paramount. The majority of franchise sales and development teams are highly reliant on their Customer Relationship Management (CRM) systems to achieve this important task. Thanks to today’s technologies, now fully integrated with the power of AI, CRM databases have become much more complex in both design and functionality. Much thought and consideration go into these factors, providing development teams with what they hope will be an efficient roadmap to meet their outsized recruitment goals.

But there’s a catch. Regardless of how much time, capital and manpower sales and development teams pour into their proprietary CRM systems, they’re not exactly infallible. And that’s where the true issue lies. Accepting this reality means you understand the limitations, making now as good a time as any to confront the reality of what your CRM can do.

Angler Shatters 76-Year-Old Wisconsin Smallmouth Bass Record

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Austin Schiefer caught a 9-pound, 11.5-ounce smallmouth bass from the deep waters of Green Bay, shattering Wisconsin's 76-year-old state record by more than 10 ounces. He landed the fish on a 5-inch soft jerkbait rigged to a minnow-head jig, cast 80 feet into deep water and worked with a live scope to track the fish off the bottom.

A Wisconsin angler recently crushed the state’s longstanding benchmark for smallmouth bass. Austin Schiefer caught the near 10-pound smallie while fishing in the deep waters of Green Bay with a 5-inch soft jerkbait, he tells Field & Stream. The fish dethroned a Wisconsin state record that has stood for three quarters of a century.

Schiefer was fishing from a boat with his friend Ted Johnson when he hooked into the enormous smallmouth. They’d been catching smallies on tubes all day, he says, when they decided to switch up baits. “Ted told me to come up to the front of the boat to get in on the action,” he recalls. “I switched to a stouter rod with a jerk bait rigged to a minnow-head jig and made an 80-foot cast.”

Through the live scope, Schiefer watched a giant blob come off the bottom and follow his bait for 30 or 40 feet, he says. Then the blob engulfed his bait. “When I set the hook I thought is was a big drum because it was rolling on the bottom,” he says. “Then as it got a little closer, it started fighting more like a smallmouth—shaking its head and taking drag.”

The giant smallmouth circled the boat then hesitated on the port side before Johnson netted it on his first try. “Once we got it in the boat we both guessed that it was easily over 7 pounds,” he says. “But the more I looked at it, the more shocked I was.”

On the digital scale inside the boat, the fish registered an astonishing 9.6 pounds. “That’s when I knew I had a state record,” Schiefer recalls, “and I started calling the DNR.”

Back at the boat ramp, Schiefer took the fish to a local bait shop. The employees there directed him to a nearby grocery store with a certified scale where the fish was weighed in again at 9.72 pounds or 9 pounds, 11.5 ounces.

“I was in shock after that, and I’m still in shock,” Schiefer says. “I borrowed a cooler from my grandpa, who lives in the area, so we could keep it over night. Then I met up with two guys from Wisconsin DNR the next morning to make it all official.”

Read Next: 11 Giant Freshwater Fish that You’ve Probably Never Heard of Before

The Wisconsin Department of Natural Resources approved and certified Schiefer’s smallmouth as the new state record on November 3. It beat the previous Wisconsin record, caught by Leon Stefonek in 1950, by more than 10 ounces. The IGFA all-tackle world record for smallmouth stands at 11 pounds, 15 ounces. David Hayes caught that legendary fish in July 1955 in Tennessee’s Dale Hollow Lake.

Records like this one reframe what anglers believe is possible in waters they’ve fished for years. Schiefer wasn’t targeting a record fish. He was having a good day on tubes when a bait switch and an 80-foot cast changed everything. The fish came off the bottom in deep Green Bay water, trailed the bait for 30 to 40 feet, and ate. That sequence is a reminder that trophy smallmouth don’t always live in the obvious spots, and that the angler willing to change tactics mid-session is the one who earns the story worth telling.

AI hardware startup Nominal triples valuation to $3B in 8 months

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Nominal, a four-year-old, Austin-based startup that offers an AI platform purpose-built for engineering and testing complex hardware, like aircraft, nuclear reactors, and smart factories, has raised another big round of financing that triples its valuation. On Monday, it announced a $240 million Series C at a $3 billion valuation led by Lightspeed Venture Partners and co-led by Insight Partners.

This follows the company’s announcement in March of a $155 million Series B extension at a $1 billion valuation, led by Trae Stephens at Founders Fund. That round was a “preemptive” deal — meaning an offer initiated by the investor, not the startup. Other investors in the company include Sequoia, General Catalyst, and Lux Capital.

Nominal says that it has 80 commercial and government organizations as customers. One of its key customers is Anduril, the defense company co-founded by Stephens. Nominal also won a government contract in October when the Air Force selected it for a $71.2 million Strategic Funding Increase (STRATFI) investment, which is a program that requires matching funds from outside the Air Force.

Nominal CEO and co-founder Cameron McCord (pictured) is a former U.S. Navy submarine officer and an Anduril alum.

How Fleeting Moments With Strangers Combat Loneliness

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A sociological study published in Theory and Society explores the profound psychological value of fleeting, emotionally resonant encounters between strangers. Led by Professor Joseph Hermanowicz at the University of Georgia, the research coins the term “evanescent interactions” to describe brief, unexpected exchanges of empathy, such as a reassuring smile or a supportive phrase during a moment of distress.

Far from being mere superficial pleasantries, these micro-connections validate human presence, instill a sense of belonging, and act as a critical social buffer against widespread alienation, loneliness, and institutional attrition.

Source: University of Georgia / Theory and Society

Picture navigating a crowded grocery store or farmers market with a screaming toddler tucked under your arm. Exhausted, embarrassed, and leaving empty-handed, you brace yourself for critical stares and judgmental glances from passersby.

Instead, an older stranger catches your eye, offers a warm, knowing smile, and softly says: “We’ve all been there. You’re doing great, Mama.”

In a fraction of a second, humiliation gives way to relief. You feel seen, understood, and anchored.

While these brief encounters between strangers are common in daily life, their sociological weight and psychological power have largely gone unnamed, until now. In a study published in the journal Theory and Society, Dr. Joseph Hermanowicz, professor of sociology in the Franklin College of Arts and Sciences at the University of Georgia (UGA), defines and examines the phenomenon of “evanescent interactions.”

Hermanowicz defines evanescent interactions as fleeting, highly emotionally charged exchanges in which strangers share gestures or words that generate an instant “spark” of mutual comprehension.

Crucially, these encounters transcend routine social politeness. While holding an elevator door or uttering a mechanical “good morning” maintains baseline social etiquette, an evanescent interaction carries an emotional charge that directly targets another person’s subjective state.

“What is communicated by these evanescent interactions is essentially, ‘You’ve noticed me. You’ve confirmed my existence. You’ve communicated that I am welcome here, that I belong here, that I have a place here, that I have value,’” explains Hermanowicz.

These micro-exchanges function as social mirrors, reassuring people navigating vulnerable, stressful, or solitary moments that their struggles are shared and their presence is legitimate.

“It’s important to be pleasant and kind to people, particularly in our present times, of course,” says Hermanowicz. “But I think it’s also important to recognize that when we do have a certain deliberateness with communicating with people, we could be accomplishing something very meaningful. We could communicate to people that they are important, that they matter, that we together form a community.”

The protective nature of these brief connections first emerged during Hermanowicz’s earlier investigations into higher education attrition. While analyzing why undergraduate students chose to drop out or persist in university life, he uncovered a recurrent social theme.

Former students who left university environments frequently cited feeling invisible, anonymous, and unmoored within large campus bureaucracies. Many noted that a single meaningful encounter with a faculty member, staff worker, or peer who demonstrated genuine, unprompted interest in their well-being might have provided the sense of belonging required to stay.

“In the absence of such contact, they felt alienated and not noticed, which fueled or did not impede a desire to leave,” Hermanowicz notes. “So they left.”

In modern societies facing widespread public health warnings regarding an epidemic of loneliness, these low-stakes, high-impact interactions represent an underappreciated source of emotional resilience. Because modern life increasingly funnels human engagement into digital feeds and automated interfaces, spontaneous physical-world acknowledgment has become scarce, amplifying the psychological benefit of face-to-face micro-empathy.

“A lot of people are lonely. These sparks of connection can thwart feelings of not belonging in the various groups and organizations that we are a part of, whether those are friendship groups, work organizations or society more broadly,” says Hermanowicz.

Because evanescent interactions are fleeting by definition, society often treats them as negligible background noise. However, Hermanowicz argues that they serve as the glue maintaining trust and cohesion in diverse communities.

When two strangers acknowledge each other’s humanity, whether through a shared laugh on a delayed train, a compassionate comment to an overwhelmed parent, or a gentle nod during a stressful moment, they bridge societal divides without the demands of long-term commitment.

“Evanescent interactions are crucial to social life,” concludes Hermanowicz. “Two people communicating empathy to each other and recognizing each other as important holds extraordinary consequence. It affirms our mutual place in human community.”

Evanescent interactions are brief, positive, and emotionally charged exchanges between strangers, such as a reassuring smile, a kind phrase, or an empathetic gesture, that create an immediate spark of mutual recognition and understanding.

How do evanescent interactions differ from standard politeness or small talk?

Standard politeness (such as saying “thank you” or nodding) follows social scripts without necessarily engaging emotionally. Evanescent interactions are deliberate moments of shared empathy that communicate deeper messages of validation, signaling: “I see you, you belong here, and your presence matters.”

Why are these micro-connections important for mental health and loneliness?

In an era characterized by social isolation and institutional alienation, evanescent interactions act as an emotional buffer. They combat loneliness by affirming an individual’s place within the wider human community, helping people feel less invisible and more grounded in their surroundings.

The kindness of strangers: on the social significance of evanescent interaction

In periodic ritual patterns, strangers exchange gestures that create a “spark” between them. This article identifies a potent form of interaction that has gone under-examined, what I term evanescent interaction, which exists in the realm of informal, momentary exchange.

The article develops an ontology of the interaction which enables us to understand how it is generated as part of the interaction order of social life. While generalized as an interaction form, the phenomenon is anything but mundane.

In tracing the interaction’s form and consequences, the article theorizes a way by which its micro elements create a connection to organizational life with discernible macroscopic effects. A theory of evanescent interaction is built by incorporating the ideas of personhood and social integration on the one hand with the roles of emotion and narrative on the other.

While under-acknowledged and seemingly inconsequential, evanescent interaction thwarts fear and enables connection to human community.
