Rubies on Mars?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Written by Roger C. Wiens, SuperCam Principal Investigator for NASA’s Perseverance rover at Purdue University

Exploration on Mars turns up the unexpected. Perseverance is equipped with a fantastic set of instruments for studying the rock record of this ancient planet. Both PIXL and SuperCam have great capabilities for detecting all kinds of elements. SHERLOC, SuperCam, and PIXL can detect minerals using various techniques including x-ray fluorescence, Raman, and infrared spectroscopies. As a side benefit of the way SuperCam performs its Raman spectroscopy, it can also study time-resolved luminescence. Normally luminescence occurs over a broad wavelength range (most of Earth’s commercial lighting uses luminescence in some way or another). Two years ago, the SuperCam team was startled to find strong, narrow luminescence peaks at two wavelengths of 692.7 and 694.1 nanometers when they shot a light-colored rock with the laser. This luminescence comes from delayed atomic transitions in chromium atoms present in a corundum mineral structure. SuperCam can time the delay of the atomic transitions via the resulting photons, and so we could verify that this emission came from chromium in corundum. The results were published in Geophysical Research Letters by a team led by Ann Ollila earlier this year, so you can read all about it. The gemstone version of chromium-containing corundum is ruby. Prior to installing SuperCam on Perseverance, we placed a ruby in the laser beam and watched the entire room pulse a brilliant red as SuperCam’s laser beam hit the gemstone; it was extremely bright.

While we haven’t found collectable rubies on Mars yet, the little corundum crystals are helping us learn more about Mars’ past. Corundum is a metamorphic and pegmatitic mineral which we would not expect to find on Mars, a planet that lacks plate tectonics. However, large meteor and asteroid impacts can create local metamorphism due to long-term heating from the impact. Because we are in a very ancient terrain near the rim of Jezero crater, we can expect some evidence of localized metamorphism. As the first mission in such terrain, we are learning new things. Earlier in the mission we tried this technique only rarely; lately we have carried out the time-resolved luminescence observations frequently on light-toned rocks to look for more evidence of impact effects. It’s great to have this unexpected tool on board as we delve further into Mars’ deep past.

Dear Robot: How Travel Is (and Isn’t) Talking to AI

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Travel brands invest deeply in AI visibility, but according to Skift's audit of 50 major travel websites' crawler policies, few tell bots where to go — and the directives don't always match who actually gets in. That looks like a shaky starting point, as the rules of AI engagement change.

Travel brands say they want AI to find them. Their websites often tell AI something else, or nothing at all.

That’s the finding of Skift’s review of robots.txt files for 50 major travel sites. These files tell automated programs, or crawlers, whether they’re welcome, and only 10 of the 50 explicitly addressed AI crawlers. The rest lumped them in with every other bot or left them to default settings.

The gap matters more as AI becomes a way travelers shop. In July, Adobe reported that AI visits to travel sites surged 119% year over year. But there’s a “major bottleneck,” according to Adobe analyst Vivek Pandya. “Significant portions of U.S. travel and retail sites remain unreadable to machines, restricting their presence across AI search results,” he wrote in a blog post.

“When an LLM cannot easily read brand content (such as amenities, pricing, or availability),” Pandya added, “potential revenue is being left on the table.”

Very Online is your best source for news about online travel, from startups to the biggest online travel agencies.

By submitting this form, you agree to receive email communication from Skift.

See more essential travel news in your search results.

Tags: artificial intelligence, online travel, skift feature, skift intelligence, the prompt

Skift provides the insights. You make the decisions.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Skift reviewed robots.txt files — the file that tells automated crawlers whether they're welcome — for 50 major travel sites and found that only 10 explicitly addressed AI crawlers. The other 40 left AI access to catch-all rules or default settings written for other purposes. That gap gained weight on Oct. 30, when Anthropic, Google, Microsoft, and OpenAI backed a proposal that would let a site opt out of AI training by all four with a single robots.txt entry — but only if the site adds it explicitly, since catch-all rules won't carry it. Among the sites that did set AI rules, the pattern favored visibility over training: Expedia and Vrbo give AI search crawlers broad access, Tripadvisor blocks training crawlers while allowing some search access, and Best Western and ITC Hotels explicitly welcome OpenAI's training crawler. Airbnb made the only major change in the sample, moving from allowing GPTBot and ClaudeBot in August to disallowing access across the board by October. Posted policies also diverge from enforcement: eight sites, mainly hotels, returned 403 Forbidden errors to Skift's automated requests, and CraftedStays found Marriott, Hilton, IHG, Hyatt, and Four Seasons name no AI crawler on their banned lists while their bot protection refused fetches outright. Adobe reported in July that AI visits to travel sites surged 119% year over year, and Adobe analyst Vivek Pandya wrote that significant portions of U.S. travel and retail sites remain unreadable to machines, leaving potential revenue on the table.
